Motorola MA2 wireless Android Auto adapter is now available, but at nearly twice the price

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

After first appearing months ago, the Motorola MA2 wireless Android Auto adapter is officially available for purchase in the US, but at a much higher price than expected.

While there’s a sea of wireless Android Auto adapters on the market today, the Motorola MA1 captured the attention of many as it was backed by Google directly. That was a double-edged sword, as we mentioned in our review, but it still got the job done. Now, it’s getting a sequel in MA2.

This new adapter was first announced way back in March 2026, bringing with it some welcome upgrades to the hardware including:

You’ll also get both USB-C and USB-A cables in the box with the adapter, and it’s reiterated that the thing that makes this wireless Android Auto adapter stand out is that it’s the “only one engineered by Google.”

The new device is now available on Amazon for $69.99, and fills a gap as Motorola MA1 has been out of stock for months now. Where MA1 launched at nearly $100, MA2 is a much more reasonable price. That said, it’s not as cheap as we were expecting

Motorola MA2’s price was originally said to be $39.95, but the final price is $69.99 on Amazon. That price isn’t a complete surprise, as a listing on the Motorola Sound website has shown that price for months. Still, it’s a shame to see that the $40~ price promised won’t actually happen, and it’s equally frustrating that it effectively costs twice as much as it was said to.

As for shipping times, Motorola MA2 on Amazon is currently showing mid-late December delivery. That may vary based on your location and could end up being delivered early, too. It’s not uncommon for shipping timelines on new products to be incorrect for the first few days, and MA2 quite literally went on sale today as far as we can tell. That’s for the US, at least, as some users in other regions have been able to purchase the device for weeks, even months.

Are you picking up Motorola MA2? Our order is in, so stay tuned for hands-on coverage!

Toyota and Subaru hit the ‘sweet spot’ with affordable electric SUVs

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Toyota and Subaru’s new electric SUVs are catching on with buyers, offering various options and prices ranging from about $35,000 to $45,000, or the “sweet spot” of the US market.

The US electric vehicle market rebounded in the third quarter with 262,649 EVs sold, according to Kelley Blue Book estimates, up 6.1% from Q2 and the strongest performance of the year.

Although nearly every automaker has sold fewer EVs so far this year than in the same period in 2025, as buyers rushed to claim the $7,500 tax credit before it expired, a handful of brands are seeing considerably higher demand.

Outside of Rivian, Toyota and Subaru are the only brands that have higher year-over-year EV sales in the US through the third quarter.

Combined, Toyota and Subaru have sold nearly 50,000 EVs through the first nine months of 2026, more than double compared to the same period last year.

Toyota alone has sold nearly 35,000 electric vehicles, up 183.4% YOY, while Subaru sold 14,630, up 46.7%. And that’s not even including the additional 12,676 sold under the Lexus brand, up 137.4% YOY.

While the two Japanese automakers were infamously slow to transition to battery electric vehicles, the timing worked in their favor.

After launching the heavily updated bZ in late 2025, previously called the bZ4X, Toyota’s electric SUV suddenly became one of America’s most popular EVs. Through Q3, it was the fourth best-selling EV in the US, trailing only the Hyundai IONIQ 5, Tesla Model 3, and Tesla Model Y.

Starting at $35,000, the Toyota bZ is one of the most affordable EVs in the US. It also offers up to 314 miles of range in the FWD Plus trim, which starts at $37,980.

Toyota and Subaru now each sell three electric SUVs, which share the same platform and technology.

With prices ranging from roughly $35,000 to about $45,000, Stephanie Valdez Streaty, director of industry insights at Cox Automotive, said (via Bloomberg) Toyota and Subaru’s new EVs are affordable and hit the “sweet spot” of the US car market.

Toyota also sells the smaller, sporty C-HR, starting at $37,080, and the larger, more rugged bZ Woodland, which starts at $45,380.

Subaru, on the other hand, sells the updated Solterra ($38,495, plus the smaller Uncharted ($34,995) and midsize Trailseeker ($39,995).

Karla Fisher, a Marine and retired engineer, told Bloomberg that the Subaru Trailseeker was her “most favorite car of all time.”

While she considered buying an EV before the $7,500 federal credit expired last September, none met her requirements at the time. Fisher wanted at least 7″ of ground clearance, 250 miles or more range, and a price that was similar, or even less than a comparible gas-powered vehicle.

The Subaru Trailseeker offers all of that and starts at under $40,000. Toyota and Subaru’s electric SUVs all start at about $45,000 or less, which is even less than the average new vehicle.

According to the latest Kelley Blue Book figures, the average price for a new vehicle in the US reached a record $51,109 in October, up 2.1% YOY.

Subaru spokesperson Dominick Infante said the affordable EV prices are mainly thanks to its partnership with Toyota. “We do believe there’s an EV future,” Infante added.

Next year, both brands will add a three-row electric SUV with the Toyota Highlander EV and Subaru Getaway.

Thinking about making the switch to an EV? With the 2027 models at dealerships, now could be a good time to find a deal on current 2026 models. You can use the links below to see what’s available at Toyota and Subaru dealerships near you.

Pompliano Says Bitcoin Spot ETFs Are Safer and More Convenient Than Hardware Wallets for Bitcoiners Holding Over $10,000

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Pompliano’s comments on Bitcoin hardware wallets and Bitcoin ETFs come at a time when more people show interest in BTC amid security threats.

Bitcoin expert Anthony Pompliano today tackled a crucial question that serious Bitcoin investors often ask. The question is: between hardware wallets and ETFs, which one offers the best outcome for securing and getting exposure to Bitcoin?

In an interview released early today, the celebrated crypto analyst answered questions savvy investors want answered about Bitcoin self-custody versus Bitcoin ETFs.

Today, Bitcoin adoption grows at a remarkable rate. As more investors seek to invest in the cryptocurrency, there are two major ways to do so: gaining exposure through Bitcoin hardware wallets (self-custody) or Bitcoin ETFs.

In the podcast, Pompliano admitted that using spot ETFs is more secure than storing Bitcoin in a hardware wallet. According to the crypto expert, spot ETFs offer an accessible approach, providing investors with a simplified, regulated way to buy and invest in Bitcoin through their existing brokerage accounts. As Pompliano stated, ETFs stand out because they emphasize the importance of regulation in providing investor protection and market integrity.

Pompliano further recognized spot ETFs as the safest and the most convenient option for investors holding between $10,000 and $10 million worth of BTC. He said these investment vehicles offer greater liquidity than directly holding BTC on hardware wallets. They provide investors with diversified exposure to stock exchanges and on-chain markets.

However, he said convenience comes with caveats, as ETFs provide indirect exposure to Bitcoin. This means that investors don’t actually own the underlying Bitcoin. Also, ETFs might not provide investors with the same level of control as directly owning BTC in self-custody wallets.

This highlights the advantages of Bitcoin hardware wallets, which allow customers to hold BTC directly, giving them greater control. However, this level of control comes with the responsibility of managing private keys, asset control, and security risks.

The debate between self-custody and ETFs comes at a time when the crypto industry continues to experience increasing security breaches, with the latest and largest one being the Binance infiltration.

Such unfortunate hacking events compel customers to rethink how to secure their BTC while still accessing strong liquidity. The self-custody approach offers direct ownership and control of BTC, though it requires technical know-how and involves greater security risks. On the other hand, ETFs offer a simple, regulated way to gain exposure to the asset, with diversified liquidity. They, however, come with some limitations.

Art Week NYC Kicks off at Metropolitan Museum of Art with New York Pols and Art Leaders

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Dozens of leaders from the New York art and political worlds rubbed shoulders at the Metropolitan Museum of Art on Tuesday morning at the launch of Art Week NYC, a citywide gathering celebrating New York’s leading galleries, institutions, and cultural spaces, organized by ARTnews, Art in America, and Artforum. The inaugural event takes place from November 11–14, 2026.

The distinguished roster of speakers included Julie Menin, City Council Speaker; City Council Deputy Speaker Nantasha Williams; Manhattan Borough President Brad Hoylman-Sigal; New York State Senator Jose Serrano; and City Council member Virginia Maloney. Related Articles After Galliano Firestorm, Met Plans New Costume Institute Exhibition Unlikely to Offend Anyone The Met Museum and Vacheron Constantin Unveil Artworks Produced Through New Residency

In attendance were VIPs like advisor Augusto Arbizo; MoMA PS1 director Connie Butler; Lisson Gallery international director Alex Logsdail; Arts Council England chairman Nicholas Serota; and Simone Subal, senior director at Paula Cooper Gallery, along with ARTnews/Art in America editor Sarah Douglas and publisher Erica Lubow Necarsulmer as well as Artforum co-editor Rachel Wetzler and publisher Danielle McConnell.

The gathering took place in the light-filled Charles Engelhard Court, anchored by the Neoclassical facade of the Second Branch Bank of the United States (1822–24) and featuring sculptures by American artists such as Daniel Chester French, Hiram Powers, and Augustus Saint-Gaudens.

All were there, said David Breslin, the museum’s curator in charge of the modern and contemporary art department, “to launch what we know will become a defining tradition in our city’s cultural landscape—and that’s saying a lot.”

Kicking off the remarks, City Council Speaker Julie Menin looked to history to set the tone, noting that it was 100 years ago when pioneering New York dealer Edith Halpert launched what would become the Downtown Gallery, which was early to show American modern art and folk art. To signal its spirit of inclusivity, Menin said, it was initially called Our Gallery, adding, “As the very first citywide contemporary art festival, that is precisely the same spirit that Art Week NYC has built here.” The City Council was proud to allocate a record $323 million in its latest budget to the Department of Cultural Affairs, she added.

Manhattan Borough President Brad Hoylman-Sigal recalled bringing together all the city’s local community boards at the museum recently and finding that some had never before visited. “Art Week is a way to invite New Yorkers—just like we invited those community board members to the Met to experience art—to meet New Yorkers, as they say, where they are,” he said, and called New York’s hundreds of galleries, many of them participating in Art Week, “magnificent little crown jewels.”

Citing Public Theater founder Joseph Papp to the effect that art belongs to everyone, Hoylman-Sigal noted, “I’m very proud to have committed 100 percent of our capital dollars to arts and cultural institutions.” That was $50 million in discretionary funds, he noted, adding that the recipient institutions were called upon to leverage the public dollars to raise money from private donors, and the 83 cultural institutions raised an additional $52 million through what he called the “Manhattan Multiplier.”

Wrapping up the event, City Council member Virginia Maloney noted that her namesake grandmother was an artist before it was common for women to have careers. Her brother-in-law is also an artist, she noted, adding, “They both taught me that art changes the way that you view the world. As chair of the Economic Development Committee, I also know that culture and art is not a luxury in New York. It’s an economic engine for tourism, for visitors, for employers, and for businesses that surround the organizations that we’re supporting today. When we invest in the arts, we know that we recoup the benefits tenfold.”

For a full listing of member galleries and institutions and a calendar of events, head to Art Week NYC.

Innovators at Work: The Price for Breaking the Rules

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 11, 2026 | Reviewed by Davia Sills

Ethical disruptive innovators identify and solve problems through prosocial and creative means that often unsettle group norms.

Disruption can be interpreted both as destructive and divine, depending on the professional culture. For example, part-time Sherwin-Williams employee Tony Piloseno, a senior at the time at Ohio University, used his popular TikTok channel to share his paint-mixing acumen, a clever contribution meant to bolster the company’s products, yet instead of accolades, he was served termination papers for violating company policy. However, instead of accepting one of the multiple job offers spurred by the news coverage, he poured his energy into starting his own paint company, a bold innovator’s move (Tonester Paints, n.d.).

Organizational norms govern how employees typically behave at work, but norms can be messy business, for institutions possess multiple forms. There are injunctive norms, which are the “ought to” behaviors, and descriptive norms, which detail what people actually do. When a gap exists between the two, the Focus Theory of Normative Conduct suggests we follow the norms we see others live out (Cialdini et al., 1991).

For example, let’s say that during faculty meetings, university administrators regularly profess that they value employee well-being (injunctive norms), yet faculty workload dictates 70-hour work weeks, and the university gym is free to students, but not to employees. Faculty will work longer hours and exercise fewer hours, putting their health at risk, because the descriptive norm is what plays out on campus despite injunctive norms to the contrary (Cialdini et al., 1990; Roswag et al., 2026; Skogstad et al., 2017).

We are shaped by those with whom we share our social spaces, so what happens when an employee decides to think past tradition, explore new ideas, and forge unique connections in a quest to innovate novel solutions to organizational problems (Lawande, 2023)?

Dyer and colleagues (2011) studied just that, interviewing creators of groundbreaking products and founders and leaders of visionary companies. They shared their insights in the book The Innovator’s DNA: Mastering the Five Skills of Disruptive Innovators. Across their interviews, surveys, and research, five characteristics bubbled up from their data, finding that innovators passionately practice:

Another intriguing finding was that, though many companies’ leaders facilitated processes that encouraged innovation, they themselves did not engage in innovative work. Conversely, in highly innovative organizations, making up just 15 percent of Dyer et al.’s (2009) research, top executives were among the ones actually doing, not delegating, innovation. Therefore, the injunctive (ought to) and descriptive (actually doing) organizational norms were in alignment, thus providing a clear pathway for others to join in or initiate creative work.

Again, we tend to emulate what we see, and leaders set that agenda. But what happens to innovative employees in organizations where leadership views their innovator’s DNA as a threat, not an asset?

Ethical disruption awakens innovation, but as Tony experienced, shaking trees has its costs; keepers of the status quo sometimes go to considerable lengths to neutralize norm-breakers. Norm violations are categorized as either constructive, such as crafting innovative products that surpass current offerings, or destructive, like purposefully denigrating a colleague’s reputation. The disruptor’s intentions and the innovation’s success also factor into how the norm is classified (Bennett et al., 2024). Though organizations often profess a desire for “out-of-the-box” thinkers, many nonconformists face roadblocks and repercussions. Whether the disruptor receives praise or pushback is, in large part, reflective of leadership.

According to McGregor’s (1960) seminal book, The Human Side of Enterprise, leaders adopt one of two theories when developing their style of management. Leaders with a Theory X view of management assume employees are lazy and shirk responsibility; therefore, they must be micromanaged, leaving little room for innovation. In contrast, leaders who view management through a Theory Y framework believe work can be fun, employees are internally motivated to do a good job, and that when given autonomy and resources, they can and will engage in creative work. Thus, under Theory Y leadership, innovators innovate.

Another way to think about leadership approaches that stymie innovation is through what researchers call control appraisal. Leaders with a low control appraisal struggle with self-doubt and believe they have limited ability to control outcomes. This group is unsettled by innovators, viewing them as unpredictable and destabilizing. Thus, they are quick to shut them down, often using withdrawal of opportunities and ostracization as containment strategies. By contrast, leaders with high control appraisal are self-confident and possess a strong sense of personal agency, or trust that they have the skills and fortitude to impact outcomes. These leaders are intrigued by innovators and work to form genuine connections, believing disruptors’ creativity is an asset, thus supporting and celebrating their work (Peng et al., 2026).

Social comparison theory is another helpful tool for considering leaders’ level of support for ethical disruptive innovators. It posits that leaders with a low core self-evaluation, or a propensity to question their ability to steer the ship, often become jealous of employees who exhibit high leadership potential (HLP), worried their star might surpass their own, and thus attempt to eliminate the competition by withdrawing opportunities. Conversely, comparison is minimal to nonexistent in leaders with a high core self-evaluation, and this positioning calls them to support innovative projects and proposals with the potential to bolster the institution’s success and more effectively serve communities (Yu et al., 2025).

Lastly, when faced with ethically disruptive innovators who don’t conform to the prevailing work culture, knowingly or unknowingly breaking group norms, leaders tend to react in one of five ways: forgive, reward, punish, ignore, or manipulate. According to research, however, only the reward reaction ensures the creative continues to engage in innovative behaviors. So, when a leader shuts down an innovator, the employee and the organization both lose out (Lin et al., 2016).

So, what does this all tell us about the types of leaders innovators need to engage in trailblazing work? First, innovators need leaders who clearly articulate and live into their mission, ensuring their injunctive norms (ought tos) and descriptive norms (what people actually do) are in alignment. Second, innovators benefit from leaders who build authentic relationships with their employees, not just rule-followers in their inner circle. Third, innovators thrive when working for leaders who personally engage in innovative work, and by that example, the institution develops a collective culture of creativity and care.

Bennett, R. J., Galperin, B. L., Wang, L., & Shukla, J. (2024). How <i><em>Marty Supreme</em></i> Costume Designer Miyako Bellizzi Really Shops

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The consummate downtown girl joins Bazaar’s podcast to talk about collecting vintage skirt suits and Supreme hoodies

The Good Buy is a podcast from Harper’s Bazaar in which editors Leah Chernikoff and Lynette Nylander invite celebrities, designers, models, and tastemakers to talk shop: what they buy, where they got it, and why it matters. Come down the fashion rabbit hole and take a peek inside the closets and shopping carts of the world’s most stylish people. Learn how they use style to tell their stories.

From Uncut Gems to Marty Supreme, Oscar-nominated costume designer Miyako Bellizzi is responsible for dressing some of the most memorable films of the past decade. A longtime collaborator with the Safdie brothers—Joshua and Benjamin—she’s brought the filmmakers’ stories to life with detail-oriented styling that feels lived-in and real. Whether capturing the ostentatious wardrobe of a 2012 Diamond District hustler or crafting 1950s-accurate ensembles for over 3,000 background actors in the Timothée Chalamet–led ping-pong drama, she’s proven time and time again she has a knack for storytelling.

In her personal life, this expertise has translated into an eclectic closet full of vintage finds. Known for mixing high-end designer pieces with streetwear pulls and 1940s workwear, she dresses herself with as much intention as she does the characters you see on screen. Showing up to film this week’s installment of Harper’s Bazaar’s The Good Buy, she paired a fitted blazer from her collection of 1940s and ’50s skirt suits with shorts she made herself, Phoebe Philo flats, and sculptural silver statement earrings.

“I love costume design, I love fashion, I love film, I love it all,” she said. “I love people, so I think that’s why.”

In the episode, Bellizzi dives into everything from costuming the screen to shopping for herself. Read on for highlights and watch the show in full above.

“I’ve always loved vintage, I think my parents put me onto it. My grandma had all these clothes from New York from the ’50s and ’60s that she didn’t want, and my dad is a big vintage buyer. Even my Barbies were vintage Barbies. But I also just appreciate well-made pieces, I appreciate good fabrics, things that you can’t find today. As a designer, it’s fascinating to see how things were made back in different eras.

“I collect vintage 1940s and ’50s skirt suits. It’s always been my favorite era, which was why Marty Supreme was a dream film for me. I think I started collecting in high school—we used to have vintage fairs in San Francisco, where I’m from. I like the shape and silhouette. This is what Mugler referenced. I’m a historical person and I love the shapes and silhouettes of Mugler, and I have a lot of Mugler, but also I know where it comes from, so I like to go to the source.”

“I don’t personally buy a lot of contemporary brands for myself, other than Phoebe Philo and the Row. I [shop there for] very classic basics, because I find all the freaky weird things vintage.”

“When I was a kid, I was like 5’9” in the fifth grade. I was really tall. And we’d always have to shop in the boy’s section. When I first started, I was at Details Magazine. It was my first internship; that’s why I moved to New York. But I always felt out of place. I never felt like I fit in with the fashion girlies of that time. And so I remember enjoying menswear and learning about menswear and tailoring, and I found that was more inspiring to me than being in the women’s fashion closet.”

“I was at Vice and I was doing all the fashion stories, and there was a mass exodus and it was the group that left that asked if I would be interested in doing a film. I had never done one before, and I was like, ‘Sure, I’ll try.’ Then, [with the Safdie brothers,] we had mutual friends. The film industry is very small. Everything is timing. I never thought I wanted to be a costume designer; this was not the goal when I first moved to New York. But I love it because it’s this family—you travel with your crew and you’re doing these jobs together and you live together. You’re living and breathing this alternate reality. All you’re thinking about are these characters. It’s almost like how an actor has to get into character. You have to become the character you’re creating.”

“It was tough, honestly. You have to think about yourself being presented in that way. Traditionally, most costume designers aren’t fashion girlies. I was excited for it but it was also very stressful. I remember I was working in Paris at the time and I was staying with [designer] Gabe [Semeraro]. I was freaking out. I went to a few vintage archives there, being like, ‘At least I’m in Paris. I can find a dress.’ And I did find a really beautiful red Galliano dress that I loved that I was going to rent, but I was like, ‘I don’t know, it’s so expensive.’ One night, I was home with Gabe, and he was like, ‘Babe, I’m just gonna make one for you.’ It was one of my favorite dresses I’ve ever worn or seen.”

“Every time I wrap a film, it feels like an achievement. I remember I was doing a film in Hawaii, and when I was there, I loved that the women wore their wealth on their wrists. They stack solid gold bangles [on their wrists], and most of times they have names of people in their families [engraved]. And they’re the most beautiful things. I figured out where to find them—at a flea market—and I got one and had it engraved. Now I’ve turned it into a thing where I buy myself a piece of jewelry or something really nice after I wrap a film."

“I am very thoughtful about what I buy for myself. I never want to spend too much money on myself. But I have made a folder on my phone of pieces I regret not buying. We were in Paris and went to Alaïa and I wanted this pink set that I didn’t buy and I still think about it.”

“Now that we are approaching fall, I live in a long sleeve. That’s my Bay area [upbringing] coming out of me. I need to have a XXXL long-sleeve T-shirt, heavy cotton. That is my comfort layer. I bring one everywhere. That and a hoodie. Those are my two hometown pieces. And when I get obsessed with something, I find a lot of them. I have a lot of Supreme hoodies, although my new favorites are 6397, because they have fun colors.”

“I was thinking about what I would buy if I had all the money in the world. It’s a pair of cuffs—or, actually, anything from this jewelry designer Art Smith. It’s kind of modernist jewelry from a different era, like 50 years ago. So something like that. Sophie Buhai is kind of the modern-day equivalent. Pieces that could be in museums.”

“I’m going to be realistic—I did buy four pairs of shoes this week: Chanel flats [from Matthieu Blazy’s first collection] on the RealReal, a pair of [Michael Rider’s] Celine jazz shoes, two pairs of Bottega woven flats.”

Six goals in 11 consecutive wins: Ex-Norwich City man stars for European club after £14m sale

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Norwich City bid farewell to Borja Sainz in the summer of 2025, following a fruitful two-year stay with the Norfolk outfit. NCFC signed the Spaniard from Turkish club Giresunspor and he enjoyed a steady first season with eight goals and two assists in 39 outings.

However, it was the 2024/25 campaign when Sainz would emerge as a real Championship star. The winger scored 19 goals and laid on four assists in 43 appearances for the Carrow Road club that season, prompting significant transfer interest before Porto paid a little over £14m to bring him in.

Sainz is now in his second season with the Portuguese giants. And, just like he did at Norwich City, the 25-year-old has grown into a starring role in his sophomore year.

Sainz had a solid debut year at Porto after joining from Norwich City, managing seven goals and six assists in 47 games across all competitions. He was not a nailed-on starter in Liga Portugal with 12 of his 30 league outings coming from the bench, but he settled well.

He has had to wait for his chances this term too, with all of his first eight league appearances coming off the bench despite scoring in three of those outings. Sainz has started the last three in the Portuguese top-flight though, and he has rewarded Francesco Farioli’s faith with three goals and an assist.

The ex-NCFC man has scored in back-to-back-to-back Liga Portugal games against Gil Vicente, Vitoria Guimaraes and Estoril, providing an assist against the latter too. He now has six goals and an assist to his name in 11 league matches this term.

Porto have won all 11 of those games to move into a commanding position at the top of the table. Sainz has made four appearances in the UEFA Champions League as well, but is yet to get off the mark in that prestigious competition.

Norwich City have had some real hits in the transfer market in recent times. Like Sainz, Gabriel Sara starred at Carrow Road and ended up earning a big move to Europe, while Mo Toure and Pelle Mattsson have become highly influential players at Carrow Road.

However, in terms of the money made and the level of success he found, Sainz takes some beating. He joined for nothing from Giresunspor and was sold for over £14m, making for a sizeable profit.

He’s gone on to prove his abilities after NCFC too, emerging as a key scorer at Porto. Sainz will be eager to keep delivering as Farioli and co see how long they can retain their perfect start to the Liga Portugal campaign.

Heaven’s Gate Cult Thriller ‘The Leader,’ Starring Vera Farmiga, Tim Blake Nelson and Jim Parsons, Bought for U.S. by Dark Sky Films (EXCLUSIVE)

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

On the 30th anniversary of the Heaven’s Gate tragedy — the infamous mass suicide involving members of a Californian religious cult — a film detailing the story is hitting cinemas.

“The Leader,” a true crime cult thriller starring Vera Farmiga, Tim Blake Nelson and Jim Parsons, has been picked for U.S. distribution by by Dark Sky Films, which plans to release the film in late March 2027, times to the 30th anniversary. Related Stories 'You've Got Mail' Reboot in the Works at Warner Bros. Pamela Abdy Delivers Fiery Speech After Warner Bros. Exit: ‘It Is Getting Harder, Not Easier, to Be a Woman in the Business’

From writer-director Michael Gallagher and also starring Grace Caroline Currey, and Simon Rex, the film had its world premiere at this year’s Tribeca Film Festival, landing critical praise. Variety’s review said it was the “most potent drama about life inside a cult since Martha Marcy May Marlene.”

The Exchange/Voltage is handling the remaining territories at AFM, with multiple major territories already sold to theatrical distributors.

As per the description, “The Leader” is set against the backdrop of “one of the most bizarre and devastating chapters in American history.” The story follows Marshall Herff Applewhite (Nelson), a former music teacher, and Bonnie Lu Nettles (Farmiga), a nurse whose chance meeting in the early 1970s sparks an extraordinary spiritual partnership. Convinced they have been chosen to guide humanity toward a higher plane of existence, the pair establish Heaven’s Gate, attracting followers willing to surrender their possessions, abandon their families, and sever ties with the outside world. “As their influence grows, devotion gives way to increasingly extreme beliefs, setting the group on a path toward an unimaginable conclusion,” adds the synopsis. “‘The Leader’ is the urgent question of why — and, in answer, the film becomes both a compelling psychological study and a chilling psychological horror.”

The distribution agreement was negotiated by Greg Newman, executive vice president of Dark Sky Films, and Brian O’Shea of The Exchange/Voltage Film Sales.

“True crime and cult stories continue to fascinate audiences, and Michael has delivered an exceptional film that is as compelling as it is unsettling, with a stellar cast delivering extraordinary performances,” said Newman. “For more than 20 years, we’ve built Dark Sky Films on a commitment to bold, distinctive storytelling, and ‘The Leader’ is a remarkable addition to that legacy. We couldn’t be prouder to bring this film to audiences.”

Added Justin DiPietro, executive vice president of MPI Media Group: “‘The Leader’ is an exceptional film whose critical acclaim speaks to both the strength of its storytelling and its broad audience appeal.”

Gallagher spent more than a decade researching Heaven’s Gate, drawing on historical records and archival footage to explore the psychology of cult influence, faith, and the human need for belonging.

“Heaven’s Gate has been an obsession of mine for nearly thirty years. When I was eight-years-old, 39 people died in my neighborhood. This event had a profound impact on my life. I wanted to make a film that used humanity to tell a real-life horror story— one that grabbed you and never let go,” he said. “I’m thrilled to partner with Dark Sky as we bring ‘The Leader’ to theaters nationwide.”

Said Hamza Ali, President of MPI Media Group: “The Leader’ is a powerful example of the kind of bold, thought-provoking filmmaking we want to bring to audiences. Michael has taken one of the most notorious stories in American history and created a gripping film that goes beyond the headlines to explore the people and psychology behind it. We’re excited to bring this extraordinary story to the big screen.”

“The Leader” is written and directed by Gallagher and produced by Jana Gallagher, Michael Wormser, Michael J. Gallagher, Joel David Moore, and Matt Murphie. Executive producers include Eric Gozlan and Ben Gozlan from Goldrush Entertainment, Norman Light, Evan Silverberg, James Cullen Bressack, Kelly Lynn Reiter, Stacy Kemp, Danielle Vasinova, Brian O’Shea, Sarah Dunn, Alexandra Cocean. The film features cinematography by Ben Mullen, production design by Tye Whipple, costume design by Zachary Sheets, editing by Joel Jay Blacker, casting by Lisa Essary CSA, and an original score by acclaimed horror composer Joseph Bishara. “The Leader”is produced by Cinemand Films, Balcony 9 Productions, Gold Rush Entertainment, Magnetic Labs, LB Entertainment, Red Barn Films, Copper Island, Silent Echo Productions, Drama Free Films and Entertainment 360.

May El-Toukhy’s Venice Golden Lion Winner ‘Woman Unknown’ Gets U.S. Deal During AFM

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: In a deal closing up at the American Film Market, Cohen Media Group has acquired U.S. distribution rights to Woman Unknown, the acclaimed feature from Danish filmmaker May el-Toukhy (Queen of Hearts), which won the Golden Lion for Best Film at Venice Film Festival, as well as the Volpi Cup for Best Actress for Mathilde Arcel.

The period drama, which has also been selected as Denmark’s official entry for Best International Feature Film, is set against the turbulent aftermath of World War II, and explores themes of ambition, identity, survival and the consequences of buried secrets. Related Stories News VMI Launches AFM Sales On Manila-Set Action Pic 'Big Tiger' With Luke Goss & Katherine McNamara News Leah McKendrick To Follow 'Voicemails For Isabelle' With "Ferociously Female" Thriller 'Delulu' Starring Maddie Ziegler; XYZ & Temple Hill Produce -- AFM

The acquisition was negotiated between Robert Aaronson, Executive Vice President of Cohen Media Group, and Susan Wendt, Managing Director of TrustNordisk, which is handling international sales.The official synopsis reads: “Marie, a young nanny and housemaid, is about to marry the wealthy older widower, Christian, for whom she is working. But Marie guards a shameful secret. During World War II, she was in an intimate relationship with a German soldier. Now Marie’s past threatens to catch up with her, and she is forced to do everything in her power to preserve her new status as lady of the house in post-war anarchy.” Watch on Deadline

The film has also played at the Toronto International Film Festival, BFI London Film Festival, Riga, Busan, Valladolid and Marrakech, among others. It is also on the fiction shortlist for the European Film Awards.

The film is directed by May el-Toukhy and written by el-Toukhy and Maren Louise Käehne. It is produced by Mikael Rieks (Land of Mine) for Nordisk Film Production A/S, in co-production with Nafta Films, Nordisk Film Production Sweden and Film i Väst.

Nordisk Film Distribution is handling local distribution, while TrustNordisk represents international sales.

Robert Aaronson, EVP of Cohen Media Group, stated: “May el-Toukhy has created an extraordinary film that combines the sweep of a historical drama with the intimacy and emotional complexity of a deeply personal story. Woman Unknown is a powerful exploration of survival, identity and the choices people make when confronted by their past, anchored by a remarkable performance from Mathilde Arcel. Its success at Venice and the recognition it has received internationally speak to the film’s exceptional artistry. We are thrilled to partner with TrustNordisk to bring this compelling work to American audiences.”

Susan Wendt, Managing Director of TrustNordisk, added: “We are really looking forward to our cooperation. Cohen’s enthusiasm for the film is very motivating and means a lot, and I am confident that they will do an excellent job of bringing the film to the market and to the American audiences.”

The filmmaker’s 2019 drama Queen of Hearts (2019) premiered at the Sundance Film Festival and received numerous international honors, including the Audience Award at Sundance, the Nordic Council Film Prize and three awards at the Göteborg Film Festival. El-Toukhy has also directed episodes of TV series including The Legacy, Ride Upon the Storm, Cry Wolf and Netflix’s The Crown.

Previously sold territories for Unknown Woman include UK and Ireland (Vue Lumière) Germany and Austria (Weltkino), France (Memento), Spain (Karma Films), Poland (Best Film), Portugal (Nitrato Filmes), the former Yugoslavia (MCF Megacom Film), Switzerland (Filmcoopi), Bulgaria (Beta Film), Italy (Lucky Red), Benelux (September Film), Latin America (California Filmes), Greece and Cyprus (Neo Films) and the Baltics (Estin Film).

The production received support from the Danish Film Institute, Eurimages – Council of Europe, the Investment and Development Agency of Latvia, Riga City Council, Nordisk Film & TV Fund, DR, the Swedish Film Institute, SVT, LIAA, Riga Film Fund, TrustNordisk and Zentropa.

Jeremy O. Harris EPs ‘American Theater,’ About a Canceled MAGA Theater Director’s Bizarre Attempted Comeback — Watch the Trailer

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Can a conservative “canceled” theater director rise from the ashes of his own ruination — and in this climate? That’s one of the questions top of mind for filmmakers Nicholas Clark and Dylan Frederick, whose provocative, Christopher Guest-style nonfiction film “American Theater” (except it’s a docu-, not mockumentary) is rolling out in select theaters now. A Slamdance Grand Jury Prize winner for documentary in 2025, the film is also executive-produced by Jeremy O. Harris.

“American Theater” follows Brian Clowdus — openly gay, conservative, and formerly of the Serenbe Playhouse in Georgia, who was taken down in 2019 over allegations of racism, misconduct, unsafe working conditions, and a gamut of other accusations. Clark and Frederick follow Clowdus, now a MAGA Republican, and a troupe of other conservative actors to an abandoned cabin in middle-of-nowhere Georgia where Clowdus plots the ultimate revenge: a community musical theater retelling of, of course, the 1692 Salem witch trials, riffing on Arthur Miller’s “The Crucible” (which, published in 1953, isn’t yet in the public domain). Related Stories Fan Edits Are Legal with Moonbeem, Distribution Network Hosts Approved Clips from Filmmakers ‘How to Rob a Bank’ Review: David Leitch’s Fun and Poppy YouTube Heist Movie Almost Gets Away with It

“This film was about a really fucked-up figure and a person with really fucked-up politics,” Harris told IndieWire over the phone from Copenhagen, where the Tony nominee is rehearsing a new play. “When you have unwelcoming and fucked-up politics, or a person who has unwelcoming, fucked-up politics at the center of the art you’re making, it can feel like potentially you’re endorsing their politics or celebrating them, especially because we don’t live in times that are deeply discursive or critical. At least having my name in the headline should be an invitation to certain people that maybe would’ve been like, ‘I’m not watching that,’ that maybe this is something for them.”

In taking on someone who was once a formidable presence in the Southern regional theater space, Harris said the filmmakers saw “this opportunity to embed themselves in the company for his comeback show. They realized there was a chance to look at the type of fringe character that utilized MAGA for the grifting machine that it is. MAGA is a magnet for a lot of displaced people, and theater has so many displaced people. Even thinking about the fact that one of the most famous people that stormed the Capitol was in the regional production of ‘Jesus Christ Superstar’ meant that there were ways in which the theater community wasn’t looking at some of the foxes among the lambs in our own community. This gave them the opportunity to be those foxes, or not even foxes, but to be flies on the wall of this maker who was exploiting a lot of displaced people.”

A recent Cobble Hill Cinemas screening of “American Theater” was moderated by journalist Ludwig Hurtado. Next up, the film will be available to rent on the Letterboxd Video Store, so locally driven screenings also aim to bring audiences to that platform.

“One of the things that’s very sad to me is that amongst the distributors, the ones that one would think have the most bandwidth and power to really uplift these microbudget filmmakers who are truly keeping the torch burning for that independent American brio that put American independent cinema on the map and right next to so many other stalwarts of world cinema, a lot of them are being, I think, more cautious about who they’re distributing,” Harris said. “Distribution costs are so large right now that I think that they don’t really know what to do with movies so small. And so Letterboxd has become this really great outlet because there’s such a vibrant community there who are just excited to see films and excited to learn that filmmakers who maybe are in circumstances similar to their own as movie watchers are making films.”

In a separate statement, Frederick said, “The film takes place in 2021, when Trump was facing the possibility of spending the rest of his life in prison, his political future was uncertain, and conservatives were batching DeSantis ‘24 tank tops. Democrats just had the best night of their lives in the midterms, and MAGA folks may find themselves at a low point, but it’s useful to remember that we’ve actually been here before.”

Clark added, “There was a moment when many people assumed the Trump era had come to an end. ‘American Theater’ tells a story of the Trump supporters during that time who were dedicated to figuring out what would come next. In this instance, their idea was an allegorical cancel culture musical about the Salem witch trials. It did not go anywhere near as planned, and our movie tells that story. But ultimately, everything that has happened to us as a nation ever since has only recontextualized what we saw within this brief moment in time.”

Trisha Paytas Reveals Jaw-Dropping YouTube Income She Made in a Week

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Trisha Paytas, who first joined YouTube in 2007, revealed the six-figure amount she recently earned over seven days posting on the platform.

Trisha Paytas is just raking in the money off her kitchen floor.

After all, the Just Trish podcast host revealed the most money she’s earned from her YouTube channel in a single week.

"This week was a good week," Paytas said on the Nov. 10 episode of Watch What Happens Live with Andy Cohen alongside fellow guest Hailey Glassman. "Maybe $100K."

Since joining the platform in 2007, the 38-year-old—mom to kids Malibu, 4, Elvis, 2, and Aquaman, 15 months, with husband Moses Hacmon—admitted that her income has always fluctuated, especially after growing her family.

"It changes so much," she explained to Polyester Zine in 2023. "Before I was married, I'd wake up and I'd be filming five videos a day or something like that. Then when I got pregnant, I had a very easy schedule–I was just doing like ASMR, whatever felt good that day. But I really hustled right before I gave birth."

In fact, she was juggling content for OnlyFans–which she posted on from 2020 to 2023–her podcast, as well as ASMR and lifestyle videos on her main channel and other social media accounts.

"I was doing that three or four days a week and I had my husband to help me so he would set up all the shots," Paytas recalled. "When I was doing it myself it was just literally just in my car or on my floor or something. Even after she was born I was filming in the hospital, after the hospital, I really hustled."

"Because that's a flow too, popularity, money, everything like that," she emphasized. "So there was a time when I was posting every single day making $100,000 versus a time I was posting every single day making $8,000."

And depending on the platform, Paytas has shifted gears on her content.

As she put it, "When something is good I just focus on it."

But the influencer knows that sometimes, you have to spend money to make money. Take, for instance, her highly produced movie recreations, like her October rendition of “Scream” from High School Musical 3 as Zac Efron's Troy Bolton.

"If I had $50,000 in my account, that's what I would spend on a video and then the amount kept going up," she explained to British Vogue in 2024. However, she also admitted, "I never saved before 2020 really. Over the course of 10 years, I've spent over $12 million."

There's also a cost to making your name by going viral.

"Be prepared for backlash for the rest of your life," Paytas added. "Going viral is cool because you can make a lot of money, but it'll stick with you forever."

New Richard Avedon Documentary Shows That Fashion Isn’t All Style—It’s Substance, Too

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

When The Devil Wears Prada 2 hit theaters this past spring, it painted an accurate and depressing picture of how fashion publishing has dwindled, its creative juices squeezed. Miranda Priestly, is no longer calling on her assistants to ring Patrick, a reference to the fashion photographer Patrick Demarchelier, or anyone of that caliber.

As someone who spent a majority of their career in that industry and had a short stint at Vogue, the publication the “Devil Wears Prada” films were based on, it’s fair to say everyone goes in with a dream of what it means to work at a fashion magazine. Often, those fantasies are informed by the photographs of Herb Ritts, Helmut Newton, and Richard Avedon, the latter of which is the subject of a new documentary by filmmaker Ron Howard. Titled Avedon, it releases on Netflix today. Related Articles Netflix Acquires Richard Avedon Documentary for 2026 Release After Cannes Debut Netflix Is Developing a Series About Frida Kahlo and Diego Rivera

As far as fashion documentaries go, it’s full of style and substance, backed up with anecdotes from Larry Gagosian, Tina Brown, Twiggy, Hilton Als, Samira Nasr, Isabella Rossellini, Calvin Klein, Avedon’s son John, and James Martin, the executive director of the Richard Avedon Foundation. Gagosian, who represents the Richard Avedon Foundation and has just opened an Avedon show in Beverly Hills, recalls going to brunch with the photographer, where all that was served was hard-boiled eggs and champagne. “I thought it was super elegant,” says Gagosian.

Using a combination of archival footage and interviews, Howard echoes the glitz and extravagance of fashion publishing’s bygone era of hefty budgets and creative freedom: Avedon’s photoshoots for Harper’s Bazaar and Vogue would last four days, and often featured models such as Lauren Hutton, Beverly Johnson, and Penelope Tree. Avedon managed to capture not just pretty faces, but the souls and secrets behind them.

Avedon grew up reading magazines such as Harper’s Bazaar, Vanity Fair, and Vogue at his father’s women’s retail business on Fifth Avenue. In them, he found the photographs by Martin Munkácsi, Edward Steichen, and Anton Bruehl.

He got his start in the 1940s, during World War II, working as a staff photographer for the United States Maritime Service, for which photographed around 100,000 faces on plain white backgrounds for ID cards with his Rolleiflex camera. “It’s existential white, the only thing you see is the geography of the face,” Avedon says in an old interview used in the film, referring to the way he spotlit his subjects against a white background.

After the war, he took courses in graphic design and photography at the New School, where he met Harper’s Bazaar’s art director, Alexey Brodovitch, who encouraged him to go to Paris in 1947 as part of the magazine’s mandate of “bringing back the glamor of pre-war Paris.”

At that publication, Avedon bore witness to a fashion revolution. In 1947, Christian Dior presented his New Look collection, an antidote to the rationed fabrics of the war, with full pleated skirts falling below mid-calf length. Avedon, who extravagantly captured five girls swirling in their full-length skirts, says that he lit each photograph he took in Paris as though he were reconstructing a Fred Astaire and Ginger Rogers film.

He recalls photographing a model crying at a railway station. The picture was met with some acerbic feedback from Carmel Snow, the renowned editor-in-chief of Harper’s Bazaar, who coined the term the New Look: “Nobody cries in a Dior hat, Dick.”

Avedon’s work for Harper’s Bazaar shaped his career, paving the way for him to become a star beyond the fashion world. In 1955, he photographed one of his most famous images, Dovima with elephants; Gagosian reveals he once sold it for $2 million. By 1957, Avedon had shot to fame like Picasso (he photographed the artist in 1958) and had become the inspiration behind Stanley Donen’s film Funny Face, in which an Avedon-like photographer is played by Astaire. In a nod to the film’s inspiration, Avedon was brought on to shoot the stills used for the film’s opening credits.

Fundamentally, Avedon was always on a mission to capture the human condition. He firstly did so with stars and starlets to illustrate that nothing is really so perfect. Howard’s documentary seems to suggest that this was all related to Avedon’s own imperfect upbringing.

In Howard’s documentary, Avedon, who died in 2004, says that his parents always presented an idealized family in their scrapbooks and often borrowed a dog as a prop to exaggerate the image they cultivated. He grew up in a stern household with a father that was emotionally absent for a big chunk of his life until they reconciled later in life.

His sister Louise, was his first muse. Avedon says in the film that her “beauty affected me,” and that all the models he chose resembled his sister with their oval faces, high cheekbones, and brunette hair. Louise was catatonic and spent more than 15 years living in a psychiatric hospital, where she died in 1968 at age 42.

“One of the great themes in his work is alienation and isolation. He wants to know what’s going on inside human beings,” says John, Avedon’s son. In times of heartache, Avedon reveled in his work—such as when his first wife, the actress Doe Avedon, left him for another man that was blonde and tall, or when his second wife, Evelyn Franklin, fell into depression, leading Avedon to start living out of his studio. “Everything diminishes,” Avedon says here. “Work does not. It’s the one thing that doesn’t turn on you.”

Maybe because his life wasn’t so pretty, Avedon’s photographs didn’t always flatter. When he shot Nancy Reagan, for example, he abided by all of her handler’s guidelines. Then, in the final image, he focused on her claw-like hands, a contrast to her perfectly, smooth face.

Sometimes to get the image he wanted, he would manipulate his subjects, as he did when he shot the Duke and Duchess of Windsor (formerly King Edward VIII and Wallis Simpson). Avedon told them that, on the way to the photoshoot, his taxi ran over a dog. This was a lie, but they seemed to believe it, and Avedon captured the droop in their smile. Simpson told Avedon that she would destroy him when the photos were published, but Avedon, who was Jewish, described the couple as “compassionate,” despite the fact that they sympathized with the Nazis during World War II.

It’s evident through Avedon’s line of work that the photographer saw the world beyond the pages of Harper’s Bazaar and Vogue. He waited seven weeks in Vietnam to photograph the American generals of the war; the residents of the American West and the Chicago 7 also counted among his subjects. The film portrays Avedon as a complicated figure, one whose contributions exceed the field of fashion photography.

It also portrays him as a complicated person, but it could’ve gone even further. Howard shies away from claims about Avedon’s sexuality that have been voiced in books such as Avedon: Something Personal, by Norma Stevens and Steven M. L. Aronson, and Michael Gross’s Focus: The Secret, Sexy, Sometimes Sordid World of Fashion Photographers. Gross reports that Avedon had an affair with his high school classmate, the writer James Baldwin, who even worked on a book with Avedon called Nothing Personal. That isn’t addressed here. (Avedon’s foundation vigorously disputed certain episodes described in Something Personal upon the book’s release, but it did not outright deny the descriptions of the photographer’s sexual identity.)

Howard also glosses over the fact that Avedon stopped working for Vogue when Anna Wintour replaced Grace Mirabella as editor-in-chief, moving away from his cropped headshot covers. This is also skipped over entirely.

Howard is instead more focused on the glamorous ghosts of a vanished era. The fashion industry has been consumed by capitalism, and fashion magazines have lost their ability to influence. All we’re left with is tales of the good old days and a graveyard of nostalgic images. You don’t have to look too closely to see that the devil is in the picture. For proof, just look at one of the photographs featured here: Avedon’s 1952 portrait of the actor Charlie Chaplin, who raises two fingers to his head, as though he were Satan himself.

Trump wanted little Kei trucks for America, Toyota makes it happen

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Does this herald the start of a small car revolution, or is it a meaningless distraction?

The idea that Americans should be offered a greater choice of smaller cars has support from strange bedfellows indeed. After all, who could forget President Trump—usually one to take a maximalist approach to any problem—announcing last year that he wanted the Department of Transportation to make Japan’s diminutive “Kei” cars legal here in the US.

Ars’ attempts to get DoT to tell us more about the potential influx of little vehicles have mostly fallen on deaf ears. But this week, there has been movement on the topic, with Toyota announcing that it will bring back the Scion brand with the KEI-01.

Here in the US, the preponderance of humongous SUVs is a consequence largely of government policies, specifically corporate average fuel economy standards that made it less profitable to sell smaller, efficient cars. Japan’s Kei cars are what happens if the government instead restricts the size, engine capacity, and power output.

A Kei car must fit within the following dimensions: 11.2 feet (3.4 m) long, 4.9 feet (1.5 m) wide, and 6.6 feet (2 m) tall, with an engine displacement of no more than 0.66 L and an output of 63 hp (47 kW) or less. That’s a far cry from the more than 16 feet (4.9 m) of a full-size pickup truck, but it makes sense for a country where you’re not allowed to buy a car unless you have somewhere to park it, with those spaces being at an absolute premium.

Being affordable to buy and economical to run sure doesn’t hurt either, and last year, Japan bought almost 1.7 million Kei cars, out of total sales of 4.6 million vehicles. Depending on where you live in the US, it can be varying degrees of difficult to import a Kei car, at least until now.

Toyota killed off its Scion sub-brand more than a decade ago, ending the haggle-free approach and cars aimed at younger buyers in favor of selling more Toyotas. But now it’s back as “Scion by Toyota,” which the automaker will apparently use to experiment with less traditional ideas. But forget about a cool-but-boxy EV in the mold of the Honda N-One or Nissan Sakura.

“The future of mobility will not be defined by one vehicle, one technology, or one way of thinking,” said Simon Humphries, Toyota’s chief branding officer and head of design. “Scion by Toyota gives us a place to challenge conventional thinking, explore new possibilities, and create mobility in new ways. The KEI-01, SxS, and BKE concepts each represent a different possibility—and together, they show what can happen when curiosity leads the way.”

Before anyone gets too excited, the KEI-01 will be classified as a “low-speed vehicle,” which means it can’t go any faster than 25 mph (40 km/h). So if you were hoping to use one of these but need to drive on anything but surface streets, be prepared to get honked at, a lot, and not in a good way. In fact, unless you’re buying a fleet, Toyota won’t sell you a KEI-01 at all, at least not to begin with.

Oh, and it’s only available as right-hand drive, since that’s the side of the road Japan drives on. But it does feature all-wheel drive, so if you own a ranch—or perhaps a whole load of golf courses—the utility becomes a little more obvious. Toyota says the KEI-01 is suited to small business owners in urban environments, farmers and ranchers, organizations that have campuses that need fleets of facility vehicles, delivery drivers, and outdoor enthusiasts. The trucks will be shipped to Toyota’s assembly plant in Indiana as knock-down kits, adding 50 jobs to the plant in mid-2027.

Low-speed vehicles have been street-legal in parts of the US for many years now. In my neighborhood in Washington, DC, Circuit operates a low-speed EV shuttle on demand, and gas-powered golf carts are very popular in retirement communities. Pricing for the KEI-01 hasn’t been announced, but speculation runs from as little as $10,000 to as much as $28,000.

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

CFTC is warning Kalshi it could face charges over contract filing failures

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Kalshi calls the issue a procedural error involving a handful of contracts, many of which were not live, and says it is addressing it

Kalshi has received a warning from the Commodity Futures Trading Commission that it could face charges over how it filed new event contracts with the agency, according to Reuters, which cited Politico. The CFTC’s notification suggests charges may be imminent.

The CFTC is examining whether Kalshi neglected to notify it about event contracts the company planned to list, Kalshi confirmed to CNBC. The company said the agency singled out contracts tied to weather, and that it is cooperating with the regulator to resolve the issue. Kalshi did not say when the investigation began.

"The notice is about a procedural error involving the submission of a handful of contracts, many of which weren't live," Elisabeth Diana, head of communications at Kalshi, said, according to CNBC. Kalshi declined to comment to CNBC on possible charges.

Reuters reported that the probe covers dozens of new products, while Kalshi described it as involving a handful of contracts.

The CFTC had not responded to requests for comment from Reuters or CNBC when they published.

Prediction market platforms may launch event contracts on their own certification, with no need for advance CFTC sign-off, provided they follow the Commodity Exchange Act, according to CNBC. That process allows new contracts to be introduced faster.

Back in August, the CFTC directed Kalshi to remove sports-related mention markets, which let traders wager on the words individuals use during a broadcast or speech, while the agency studied that contract type, according to CNBC.

Never Paid Into Social Security? Here's How You Might Qualify for Benefits Regardless

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Social Security benefits typically have to be earned, but there's another option if you don't have a work history.

Social Security is a lifeline for millions of older Americans today. And for many people, the path to being eligible for benefits involves working and paying into the system.

Social Security's primary source of revenue is payroll taxes. As such, to qualify for benefits in retirement, you need to pay taxes on a certain amount of wages to accumulate work credits.

In 2026, for example, the value of a single Social Security work credit is $1,890. In 2027, that value is rising to $1,970.

You can collect a maximum of four Social Security work credits per calendar year, and you need 40 total work credits in your lifetime to receive benefits in retirement. But if you never worked or have a minimal earnings history, you may not have those 40 credits by the time your senior years roll around.

The good news is that there may be another path toward collecting Social Security. It's important to know what options you have if your earnings history does not render you eligible for benefits.

If you're married to someone who's eligible for Social Security benefits or are an eligible divorcee, you may be entitled to collect spousal Social Security benefits. But it's important to know how they work.

First, if you're married, you can't file for spousal Social Security benefits until your spouse signs up. If you're divorced, you may be able to get your spousal benefits before your former spouse files. Your ex-spouse also can't prevent you from claiming spousal benefits, and your claim doesn't impact the amount of money they get.

Secondly, spousal Social Security benefits max out at 50% of your current or former spouse's full retirement age benefit. And you can claim your spousal benefits in full at your full retirement age.

In other words, if your spouse gets $2,000 at their full retirement age, your spousal benefit is worth $1,000 if you claim it at your full retirement age. If you file early, which you can do starting at age 62, your spousal benefit will be reduced for life.

You also can't boost your spousal benefit by delaying your claim past full retirement age. Delayed retirement credits are available to Social Security recipients claiming benefits based on their own earnings records, but they don't apply to spousal benefits.

If you never worked or barely worked, you might assume that Social Security is off the table. But you may be entitled to spousal Social Security benefits that can serve as a key source of income later in life.

The key is to make sure you understand how those benefits work, what they're worth, and when to file. The more you know about spousal benefits, the more helpful they might be to your finances.

Avalanche (AVAX) Gains 4% After Securing Major NYSE Partnership

📅 2026-11-11  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

On Wednesday, Avalanche's price recorded a 4% gain on news of the new NYSE partnership.

AVAX is now trading at $8.54, up 2.7% over the past 24 hours despite a broader crypto market decline. The token's market cap also saw an increase of 2.8%, now sitting at $3.8 billion.

Avalanche is a Layer 1 blockchain supporting decentralized applications and custom blockchain networks. It helps companies build their own infrastructure using the Avalanche network.

Over the last month, Avalanche's price has declined by more than 21%, bottoming out at about $7.82.

NYSE's plans involve using the Avalanche network as the settlement ledger for its upcoming tokenized securities platform. The launch is still in development as regulatory approval has yet to be received.

The platform will support 24-hour trading of tokenized stocks across multiple blockchains. NYSE will deploy its own custom Avalanche-based blockchain rather than using the public Avalanche network.

Tokenized assets on Avalanche currently sit at $1.7 billion, while the network has recently surpassed 1.6 billion transactions.

This deal represents a major win for institutional adoption of Avalanche. However, it does not necessarily mean more demand for the native AVAX token.

After steadily declining since early October, AVAX is finally showing some signs of trend reversal.

The $8.4-$8.5 range serves as an important support area. A breakout will pave the way for further recovery.

A drop below $8.37 would mean another fakeout, with a potential decline below the $8 level.

From Local to Global: Anshuman Jha & Sarah-Jane Dias On Advocating Animal Rights In ‘Lakadbaggha 2’ & Uniqueness Of Crafting An Indian Action Franchise Rooted In Local Marital Arts

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Deadline’s Local to Global series touched down in Bruges, Belgium, last week where we sat down with actor-director Anshuman Jha and actress Sarah-Jane Dias to discuss their upcoming Indian action title Lakadbaggha 2: The Monkey Business. The film, which had its world premiere at the Razor Reel Flanders Film Festival where it was the runner-up for the Audience Choice Award, is the second instalment of Jha’s franchise and intersects local Indian mythology with indigenous martial arts all while advocating for animal conservation.

The project is produced by First Ray Films and Matta Cinema and follows Arjun Bakshi (Jha) who, weeks before his wedding, is shattered when his fiancée (Dias) disappears while on a mission in North Sulawesi, Indonesia. Her trail leads to a deadly island where an underground fight tournament hides the auction of the last surviving Yaki monkey – forcing Arjun to fight not just for love, but for a life that cannot speak for itself. Related Stories News Deadline From Local To Global: Producers Of 'Gohan', '9 Temples To Heaven' & More Discuss How Thai Cinema Is Travelling News International Insider: Cannes Gets Cracking; Deadline Goes Local To Global; Barry Keoghan Interview

“I feel that animals are going extinct because humanity is going extinct,” said Jha. “So, the next evolution of heroes or ‘superheroes’ is going to be somebody who’s not more powerful, but somebody who will have a lot more compassion. Being ordinary is the real superpower and that was the root of the birth story of Arjun Bakshi.”

The first instalment of the Hindi-language franchise sees vegan Arjun travel to Kolkata where he turns into a vigilante after discovering an illegal underground animal trade syndicate. The latest film heads to Indonesia, where it puts a spotlight on the Yaki Monkey, whose population has been reduced from 50,000 in1990 to 4,500 today. “This is a real-world problem,” said Jha. “I feel films need to not just entertain but also have a voice, especially for those who are voiceless and this story is in essence about that.”

India has a rich martial arts heritage, particularly through Kalaripayattu, also known as Kalari, which Jha says has rarely been conveyed on screen.

“We are the second biggest film industry in the world, so technically there should be more films featuring this,” he says. “The effort was to bring a very 1980s pulp action genre fan to the screen. I’m a huge Bruce Lee fan. He’s had a huge influence on my life and a lot of who I am, what I believe in and how I live goes in alignment with the principles of martial arts.”

Dias noted the film’s unconventional approach to portraying an action hero: “Quintessentially, when there is fighting in a film, it’s over love or someone wanting to take over the world or they are some dark force, but I have never seen it done to protect animals or the environment, especially in a superhero action film and I think that’s actually really unique.”

Jha worked with master fight choreographer Kecha Khamphakdee, who is behind the iconic fight scenes in Ong-Bak, to design the combat scenes in the latest film. Tony Jaa, the star of Ong-Bak: Muay Thai Warrior makes his Bollywood/Hindi film debut in the film while Dan Chupong, another star from the Ong-Bak world, plays the antagonist in the film.

When pressed about the challenges of Indian films traveling outside its own borders, Jha stressed that he was conscious of this when making the film and hopes it’s a story that Western audiences can embrace because of its universal themes and the fact that it’s an atypical Indian independent film.

“I want this film to travel the world,” says Jha. “I feel like there’s an emotional quotient – EQ – and there’s cinema quotient. I think because we have 1.6 billion people in India, sometimes as filmmakers we get very happy with just what we have. The truth is, there are 5.4 billion people outside of India. So, there’s a huge audience out there and the cinema quotient of audiences outside of India is very different from the inside of India.”

Contemporary Beadworker Ken Williams Jr Awarded Rakow Commission by Corning Museum of Glass

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Marking the prize’s 40th year, "I Am the Hungry Cat" is the first Rakow Commission awarded for beadwork.

The Corning Museum of Glass selected Northern Arapaho and Cattaraugus Seneca artist Ken Williams Jr as the recipient of the 2026 Rakow Commission. Williams began learning beadwork at an early age on the Cattaraugus Seneca Indian Territory and later refined his technique alongside master beadworkers from his mother’s Northern Arapaho family.

Widely celebrated for his pictorial beadwork, Williams used the Commission as an opportunity to move beyond functional and wearable forms, creating a standalone wall panel. Utilizing 68 different colors from a range of historic to contemporary made beads, the framed composition features a graphic depiction of the artist’s cat, Valentino, complete with thought bubbles depicting his favorite foods as part of his “Yum Series.” In centering imagery of his cat and favorite foods detailed in bold colors and expressive lines, Williams is actually presenting a self-portrait through the work.

Now on view in the Museum’s Contemporary Art + Design Gallery, I Am the Hungry Cat, draws inspiration from Pop Art and a vibrant color palette and bridges ancestral artistic traditions with a playful contemporary sensibility, expanding the possibilities of glass as a medium for narrative art.

The Rakow Commission is one of the most prestigious annual awards for artists working in glass and supports the creation of a new work by encouraging artists to venture into technical or conceptual projects that they would have been unable to explore due to financial limitations. The annual program was endowed by the late Dr. and Mrs. Leonard S. Rakow, who were Fellows, benefactors, and dear friends of the Museum.

In its 40th year, the annual award was first presented in 1986, and recipients ranged from emerging to established artists including Te Rongo Kirkwood, Charisse Pearlina Weston, Leo Tecosky, Preston Singletary, Silvia Levenson, Klaus Moje, and Lino Tagliapietra.

Learn more at info.cmog.org/rakow-commission.

Watch the new documentary by BAFTA award-winning filmmaker Cherish ‘Chez’ Oteka on Field of Vision.

This residency at the U-M Stamps School, in partnership with the U-M Museum of Art (UMMA), offers artists a $20,000 honorarium, housing, studio space, and a culminating exhibition in UMMA’s prominent Stenn Gallery.

The Bayeux tapestry, a new biography of Edward Hopper, The Met's exhibition catalogue on Krasner and Pollock, what architecture is, and more
