Wet-weather and high-wind motorcycle riding: 10 habits for grip and visibility

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Learning how to ride a motorcycle safely in rain and wind comes down to traction, visibility and control, and these 10 techniques cover each one

Weather does not change how a motorcycle works. It changes how much room a rider has to get things wrong. On a dry, calm day, a tire has grip to spare, the road ahead is easy to read, and the bike holds its line with little effort. Rain and wind take some of that margin away, and they do it in different ways.

Rain is a traction and visibility problem. Water sits between the tire and the pavement, which reduces grip and lengthens the distance needed to stop. Spray from other vehicles and a fogged or streaked visor make hazards harder to spot. Surfaces that feel solid in the dry, such as painted lines and metal covers, can turn slick once they are wet. After a dry spell, the first rain brings oil and grime to the surface of the pavement, and the start of a storm is especially slippery as a result.

Wind is a stability problem. A crosswind pushes a motorcycle sideways, and a gust can move it off its line with little warning. Gaps between buildings, exposed bridges and the wake of a large truck can all produce sudden shifts in the air hitting the bike and rider.

Knowing how to ride a motorcycle safely in rain and wind means managing both sets of problems, often on the same ride. The techniques involved are not exotic. Most rely on restraint, preparation and paying attention earlier than usual.

The 10 slides that follow fall into three groups. The first covers control: speed, following distance, smooth inputs and braking. The second covers the road and the machine, from slippery surfaces and standing water to tire tread and pressure. The third covers wind and the rider: handling crosswinds and truck turbulence, waterproof gear, a clear visor, being seen in spray and knowing when to stop.

Each slide stands on its own. A rider can go straight to the situation at hand. None of it replaces formal rider training or the guidance in a motorcycle's owner's manual. What it offers is a practical set of habits for rain and wind, whether on a daily commute or a long trip.

The core adjustment for riding in the rain is to reduce speed and open up the gap to the vehicle ahead. With less grip available on wet pavement, a motorcycle needs more road to stop or change direction.

Speed has an outsized effect on stopping. The energy a braking system has to shed rises with the square of speed, not in a straight line. Braking distance grows the same way, so even a small cut in speed shortens it by a larger proportion. On a wet surface, that extra room can decide whether a sudden stop ends safely.

Following distance works alongside lower speed. A common way to judge the gap is to pick a fixed marker, such as a sign or a lamp post. Count the seconds between the vehicle ahead passing it and your own arrival. In wet weather, add time to whatever gap you would hold on a dry road. A longer gap also means less of the spray from the vehicle in front reaches the rider. That spray coats a visor and hides the road surface.

Take the most care in the early minutes of a storm. After a dry spell, oil and other residue build up on the asphalt. The first rain lifts that film to the surface before there is enough water to wash it away. Grip during that stretch can be lower than the wet road suggests. A shower after days of dry weather therefore deserves a slower pace than the same road gets in steady rain.

Rain also cuts visibility in both directions. A rider sees less through a wet visor. Drivers have a harder time spotting a motorcycle's narrow profile through streaked windshields and fogged side windows. Lower speed gives everyone more time to notice one another and react.

Watch the mirrors as well. Drivers behind may not adjust their own following distance in the wet. Slowing steadily and early gives them warning rather than forcing them to react to a sudden change.

Smooth, gradual control inputs are the core of wet-weather motorcycle riding. A wet tire has less grip to give, so any sudden change in throttle, steering or lean can ask for more grip than the tire has. That is how a slide starts.

The reason comes down to how a tire shares its grip. Each contact patch has a limited amount of traction. That traction is split between cornering forces and the forces of speeding up or slowing down. On dry pavement there is usually plenty left over. On wet pavement the total shrinks. A jerky input that would go unnoticed in the sun can use up what remains.

Throttle control comes first. Opening the throttle quickly can spin the rear tire, especially on a powerful bike or in a low gear. Rolling the throttle on slowly and steadily lets the tire take up the load in stages. Riding a gear higher than usual can also soften how power reaches the rear wheel.

Closing the throttle deserves the same care. Snapping it shut can cause abrupt engine braking, which shifts weight forward and can unsettle the rear tire. Rolling off gradually keeps the chassis calm. The clutch follows the same rule. Releasing it gently, particularly on downshifts, avoids a sudden jolt to the rear wheel.

Some modern motorcycles offer a rain riding mode that dulls throttle response, and some add traction control. These systems help, but they work within the grip the tire has. They do not replace a steady right hand.

Steering inputs need to be measured as well. Pressing the handlebar with steady, progressive force tips the bike into a turn without a sudden load on the front tire. Quick, stabbing pressure on the bar does the opposite.

Lean angle is the third piece. Using less lean in wet corners keeps more traction in reserve for the unexpected. Riders can shift their upper body slightly toward the inside of the turn. That lets the motorcycle stay more upright at the same speed. Picking a cornering speed before the turn begins, rather than adjusting it mid-corner, means fewer changes while the bike is leaned over.

Use both brakes in the rain, applying each one with lighter, more gradual pressure than you would on dry pavement. Start braking earlier, do most of it while the bike is upright, and treat anti-lock brakes as a backup rather than a reason to brake late.

The front brake does most of the stopping on a motorcycle. Under braking, weight shifts forward and presses the front tire into the road. That added load gives the front tire more grip. The shift takes a moment, and on wet pavement the tire has less grip to spare while it happens.

The safer method is to squeeze the front lever gently at first. Add pressure as the front end settles and the fork compresses. Grabbing the lever hard before the weight transfers can lock the front wheel.

The rear brake plays a supporting role. It helps settle the chassis and adds some stopping force. As weight moves forward, the rear tire carries less load and locks more easily. A light, steady press on the pedal is enough for most wet stops. Stamping on it invites a skid.

Timing matters as much as pressure. Braking earlier spreads the work over a longer stretch of road. Each brake can then be applied more softly. An early start also leaves room to adjust if the surface turns out to be slicker than it looked.

Do the bulk of your braking in a straight line, before a corner. A tire has a limited amount of grip to share between turning and slowing. Braking hard while leaned over asks it to do both at once. In the wet, there is less grip to divide.

Skids still happen, and the right response depends on the wheel. A sliding front wheel cannot steer the bike. If the front locks, release the lever at once and reapply it more gently. If the rear locks while the bike is traveling straight, keeping the pedal down until the bike stops is usually the safer choice. Releasing it suddenly can let the tire regain grip with a jolt and snap the bike sideways.

Anti-lock brakes, or ABS, monitor wheel speed and pulse brake pressure to stop a wheel from locking. That lets the rider keep steering during a hard stop. ABS does not shorten stopping distance on every surface, though. Total grip is still set by the tires and the road.

Some motorcycles also use linked or combined brakes, where one control applies force at both wheels. The owner's manual explains how a specific bike's system works.

Water on the discs and pads can briefly delay the initial bite after riding through heavy spray. A light application of both brakes before a planned stop clears that film and confirms the brakes respond.

Painted road markings, manhole covers, steel construction plates, metal bridge grating and rail or tram tracks all turn slippery in the rain. The safest approach is to spot them early and steer around them where possible. Cross any you cannot avoid with the motorcycle upright and stable.

Painted lines are the slick surface riders meet on almost every road. Lane dividers, stop lines, arrows and crosswalk stripes form a smoother layer on top of the asphalt. That layer gives a wet tire less texture to grip than the road around it. Crosswalks and turn arrows near intersections put that smooth surface exactly where a rider is most likely to stop or turn.

Manhole covers and utility access lids are usually made of cast iron or steel. Wet metal is smooth, and a tire finds far less grip on it than on rough asphalt. Many sit in the travel lane. Some are also set slightly above or below the road surface, which adds a bump to already poor footing.

Steel plates laid over construction trenches create the same problem across a larger area. Their edges can be raised, and a raised edge can deflect a tire. Treat a plate like a manhole cover that takes longer to cross.

Steel bridge grating, the open mesh decking found on some drawbridges and older spans, makes a motorcycle feel as though it is wandering. That sensation happens on dry grating too. In the rain, the wet metal adds a loss of grip to the wobble. Hold a steady speed and line across it rather than correcting every twitch of the front end.

Rail and tram tracks call for a specific technique. Cross them as close to a right angle as possible. At a shallow angle, the tire can slide along the wet rail or drop into the groove beside it. Either can steer the front wheel out from under the rider. In cities with streetcar lines, tracks often run lengthwise down the street. Choose a lane early so a turn does not force a shallow crossing.

The rule that ties all these surfaces together is timing. Finish braking, leaning and changing speed on the asphalt before the slick patch. Ride across it upright, then resume on the far side. Looking farther down the road gives time to pick a line that avoids most of these hazards entirely.

On wet roads, pick a lane position that keeps the motorcycle out of standing water and off the oily center strip. If a tire starts to hydroplane, hold the bike upright and steady, avoid hard braking, and let the tire regain contact on its own.

Hydroplaning happens when a tire cannot push water away fast enough. A thin layer of water builds up between the rubber and the pavement. The tire then rides on top of that water instead of gripping the road. A motorcycle has only two contact patches. That leaves it less margin than a car when grip disappears. Higher speed raises the risk because the tire has less time to clear water from its path.

Many roads are built to shed water. A crowned road rises slightly in the middle and slopes toward the edges. That design sends runoff toward the shoulder and gutter, where puddles tend to gather. Riding close to the curb in heavy rain often means riding through the deepest water on the road.

The center of a lane brings a different problem. Cars and trucks drip oil and other fluids there. The buildup is heaviest near intersections and toll booths, where vehicles sit idling. Water on top of that film makes the center strip one of the slickest parts of the lane.

The tire tracks left by traffic ahead are often a better line. Cars push water aside as they pass. Their wheel paths can carry less water than the surrounding surface as a result. On roads worn by heavy trucks, those same paths can sink into ruts that hold water. Watch the surface ahead and adjust the line as conditions change.

Puddles call for caution for another reason. Muddy water can hide a pothole, a broken pavement edge or debris. There is no way to judge depth from the saddle. Steering around a puddle is safer than riding through it. The move should be gradual, and the space beside the puddle should be clear.

If the front end feels light or vague in deep water, keep the handlebars straight and the body relaxed. Ease off the throttle gently rather than snapping it shut. Avoid steering corrections, and stay off the brakes until the tire bites again. A steady, upright motorcycle usually regains grip once it reaches shallower water.

Tires with healthy tread and correct inflation give a motorcycle its best chance of holding the road in the rain. Check both before any wet ride. Set the pressure to the figure the manufacturer specifies for that bike.

Tread grooves have a specific job on wet pavement. They give water somewhere to go, channeling it out from under the contact patch so the rubber can touch the road. As a tire wears, those grooves get shallower and move less water. A worn tire can feel fine on a dry day and struggle to clear water in a downpour.

Most motorcycle tires have tread wear indicators molded into the grooves. These are small raised bars that sit below the tread surface. Small arrows or the letters TWI on the sidewall often mark where they are. When the surrounding tread wears down level with those bars, the tire is due for replacement. A tread depth gauge gives a more exact reading than the eye alone.

Wear is rarely even. Bikes ridden mostly on straight highways tend to wear the rear tire flat across the center. That squared-off profile can make the bike feel reluctant as it tips into a corner. Look across the full width of each tire, not just the middle.

Pressure deserves the same attention. The correct figures are listed in the owner's manual. Many bikes also carry them on a label on the frame or swingarm. Front and rear pressures usually differ. Some manufacturers list a higher figure for carrying a passenger or luggage.

Check pressure with the tires cold, before setting off. Riding heats the air inside and raises the reading. An underinflated tire flexes more, steers heavily and builds up heat. An overinflated tire shrinks the contact patch that grips the road.

A pre-ride look should also cover the rubber itself. Cuts, bulges, sidewall cracking and embedded nails or glass all need attention before the bike leaves the driveway. In the U.S., a tire's manufacture date appears on the sidewall as a four-digit code showing the week and year it was made. Rubber hardens as it ages, and harder rubber offers less grip on a slick road.

Waterproof riding gear, a fog-free visor and high-visibility clothing solve three problems that rain creates beyond lost traction. They keep a rider dry and focused, able to see the road, and easy for other drivers to spot.

The outer shell comes first. Wet-weather riding gear comes in two broad styles. Some jackets and pants use a built-in waterproof, breathable membrane, either laminated to the outer fabric or fitted as a separate liner. Other riders wear standard gear and pull a one-piece or two-piece rain suit over it when the weather turns.

The junctions between pieces matter as much as the fabric. Rain tends to get in at the collar, the wrists, the waist and the tops of boots. Pant legs should sit over boots rather than tucked inside them. That way, water runs off the boot instead of into it.

Glove cuffs follow the same logic. Some designs go inside the jacket sleeve and some go over it. The goal is to stop water from running down the arm and pooling inside the glove.

Hands are where wet gear causes the most trouble. Waterproof gloves keep fingers warm enough to feel the clutch and brake levers. Soaked leather gloves can turn heavy, lose their feel and slip on the grips.

The visor is the next weak point. A helmet visor fogs when warm, moist breath meets a cold surface. Cracking it open clears the fog but lets rain hit the face and eyes.

An anti-fog treatment applied to the inside of a clean visor reduces condensation. A pinlock-style insert goes further. It is a second clear lens sealed against the inside of the visor. The trapped layer of air keeps the inner surface warm enough to resist fogging, much like a double-glazed window. The visor has to be designed to accept one.

The outside of the visor needs attention too. A clean visor sheds water better than a dirty or scratched one. Scratches also scatter the glare from oncoming headlights at night. Dark tinted visors block light a rider needs under heavy cloud or after sunset.

Being seen is the third job. Rain, road spray and gray skies make a dark-clad rider on a dark motorcycle hard to pick out. Fluorescent colors such as yellow-green and orange stand out in daylight and dull, overcast light.

Fluorescent fabric depends on daylight to glow. After dark, it does much less. Reflective panels and strips fill that gap by bouncing headlight beams back toward drivers. Gear that combines both covers day and night riding. A fluorescent vest with reflective strips, worn over an existing jacket, adds both features without replacing the jacket.

A relaxed grip and loose arms are the foundation of handling crosswinds on a motorcycle. A rider who stays loose lets the bike absorb a gust and settle back onto its line. A rider who stiffens up sends every push of wind straight into the handlebars.

Tension is the instinctive reaction. When wind shoves a motorcycle sideways, the urge is to clamp down on the bars. Locked elbows and a tight grip turn the upper body into a lever. Each movement of the torso then becomes an unintended steering input. The bike can wobble more from the rider's reaction than from the wind itself.

The lower body should do the work of holding on. Squeezing the tank with the knees keeps the rider anchored to the motorcycle. Pressing the balls of the feet onto the pegs adds stability. That frees the hands to steer with a light touch. Bent elbows and a slightly lower posture also present less of the upper body to the wind.

A steady crosswind calls for a different response than a sudden gust. In a constant wind from one side, the motorcycle needs to lean slightly into it to hold a straight line. That lean comes from gentle forward pressure on the handlebar on the windward side. The rider does not need to force it. Light, steady pressure is enough for the bike to find its balance.

Gusts are harder to manage. They arrive and disappear without warning. When a gust stops abruptly, a rider who was leaning into it can drift toward the side the wind was coming from. Staying loose lets the bike straighten up as the pressure drops. Small corrections keep the motorcycle from swinging across the lane.

Reading the landscape helps a rider anticipate gusts before they hit. Gaps between buildings, breaks in a tree line and the end of a sheltering hillside can all send sudden wind across the road. Swaying treetops, flags standing straight out, blowing dust and rippling grass show the strength and direction of the wind ahead.

Lane position adds a margin for error. Riding on the side of the lane the wind is coming from leaves more room to be pushed downwind while staying inside the lane.

Equipment changes how a bike reacts to wind. Tall windscreens, top boxes and side luggage all add surface area for the wind to push against. A fully loaded touring bike presents more side area than the same machine without its bags. Lower speeds give a rider more time to respond to each push of wind.

Big trucks, exposed bridges and the exits of overpasses are where wind tends to hit a motorcycle hard and with little warning. Give large vehicles extra room, and expect a sideways shove at each of these spots before it arrives.

A tractor-trailer at highway speed pushes a large mass of air ahead of it and around its sides. A rider passing one can feel a push away from the truck near the cab, then a change in pressure along the trailer. On a windy day, the truck also blocks the crosswind for a few seconds. The bike settles in that shelter, then takes the full force of the wind again as it clears the front of the cab.

Being passed by a truck produces the same effects in reverse. The answer is to plan for them. When overtaking, make the pass decisively rather than lingering alongside the trailer. When a truck approaches from behind, move toward the side of the lane farther from it to add lateral space. Do this only if the surface there is clean.

Rain makes trucks harder to deal with. Their wheels throw up heavy spray that can blank out a visor for several seconds. Dropping back widens the gap and lets that mist thin out before it reaches the rider. Truck drivers also have large blind spots. A rider who cannot see the driver's face in the truck's mirrors should assume the driver cannot see the motorcycle.

Bridges carry their own risk. A deck high above a river or valley has no trees, hills or buildings to break the wind. Gusts there are often stronger than on the approach road. Some bridges post high-wind warnings or fly windsocks that show the direction and strength of the air moving across the deck.

Overpasses and tunnels create a similar hazard. The structure blocks the wind briefly, and the bike can be hit sideways the moment it emerges. Open stretches of highway, road cuts through hills and the crest of a rise work the same way. Knowing where shelter ends lets a rider prepare for the gust before it lands.

Pull over when cold, fatigue or the weather itself starts to outpace your ability to control the motorcycle. Stopping at a safe spot to wait out a storm costs time. Pushing on through conditions beyond your control can cost far more.

Cold is often the first warning sign. Wet clothing draws heat away from the body. Air moving over a rider at road speed adds to that chill. Early symptoms include shivering, stiff or numb fingers and clumsy use of the controls. Hands that cannot feel the brake lever or clutch cannot apply them with precision. Once shivering becomes hard to stop, it is time to find shelter and warm up.

Mental fatigue builds in a similar way. Riding in rain or wind demands constant scanning for hazards, along with small corrections to keep the bike on its line. That workload tires a rider faster than a dry, calm ride does. Missing a turn, drifting within the lane or noticing a hazard later than usual are signs that concentration is slipping.

Some conditions call for a stop no matter how alert the rider feels. Lightning leaves a motorcyclist exposed on open roads. Hail strikes a rider directly and can cover the road surface. Floodwater hides its depth and any damage to the road beneath it. Rain heavy enough to obscure the road edge or nearby vehicles is another reason to get off the road. So are gusts that repeatedly push the bike across its lane.

Official alerts help with that call before and during a trip. In the U.S., the National Weather Service issues wind advisories and high wind warnings. In the U.K., the Met Office publishes weather warnings by region.

Where a rider stops matters too. An enclosed building, such as a gas station store, a cafe or a rest-area building, offers warmth and protection from lightning. A highway shoulder or the space under an overpass does not. Both leave a stopped rider close to fast traffic, often where drivers have limited time to see them.

Use the break to recover. Drink something warm, swap in dry gloves if you carry a spare pair, and check the forecast or radar. Ride on once your hands feel normal on the controls and the worst of the weather has moved through.

The Netherlands Has €231 Million for Cleaner Inland Ships. It Still Needs Electric Freight Corridors.

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Netherlands is preparing to spend €231.2 million over four years helping inland ships replace fossil-fuel propulsion. The first €36.93 million tender opens on November 17, 2026, and battery-electric vessels are among the eligible investments. This is a serious step toward decarbonizing freight on the waterways that link Dutch ports and Europe's industrial heartland. But the programme's most important scoring calculation rewards emissions avoided per euro of propulsion subsidy while excluding separate subsidies for the shore infrastructure on which the ship may depend. That distinction matters when the commercial product is a complete freight service, not an electric vessel sitting beside a quay.

The new Dutch tender is considerably more sophisticated than a technology demonstration fund, yet it leaves an important accounting boundary. My TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment follows that boundary from subsidized vessels to working routes, drawing on my open, 98-page Inland Freight Electrification report.

The RVO programme can support battery-electric systems, some hybrid arrangements and other propulsion technologies, together with eligible charging and refueling facilities. Its published explanation prioritizes electrification where practical. That is sensible: electric inland vessels are already operating in the Netherlands, and a subsidy should encourage the pathway that provides the required service at low emissions and acceptable cost.

The scheme also recognizes that a vessel does not operate in isolation. Applications receive points for commercial cooperation, cargo-owner agreements and the quality of supporting infrastructure arrangements. These are material criteria, not decorative promises. They are a welcome correction to programmes that fund novel equipment and assume the market, terminal and utility will somehow arrange everything else.

Yet most of the available points are tied to projected emissions reductions per euro of propulsion support. That is a consistent measure for comparing applications for individual ships. It is not an accounting of the entire investment required to turn a diesel freight route into an electric one. One barge may need access to battery exchange at both ends of its journey, reserve battery inventory, grid capacity, a crane or handling system and a dependable service agreement. Some of those facilities can serve many vessels, making it hard to allocate their cost and value to any single propulsion application.

Zero Emission Services, or ZES, has already shown what putting the pieces together looks like. Its swappable battery containers allow inland vessels to obtain charged energy without each owner purchasing and maintaining every battery required by the route. At Moerdijk, a terminal arrangement combines battery exchange, handling equipment and access to an existing electricity connection. The ZES network is extending beyond isolated demonstrations, although operating sites, planned stations and funded vessel conversions must not be counted as the same thing.

The difference between a capable ship and reliable freight is familiar to ports. Charged batteries must be available at the right time, terminals must handle exchanges without disrupting cargo work, and the energy-service provider needs enough committed traffic to finance its containers and chargers.

The Netherlands deserves credit for recognizing these dependencies. It need not discard vessel-level grants or wait until every future route is planned before making early awards. But a second programme-level measure is needed alongside emissions per propulsion subsidy: what complete, repeatable electric freight service is each funded project helping create, and which shared assets make that service possible?

This matters all the more because the freight being carried will change. Fossil-linked cargo is likely to decline as coal and petroleum use falls, and ports should not size batteries, terminals and connections around the assumption that today's bulk flows continue indefinitely. A route with strong contracted demand may justify shared charging equipment while a low-utilization route requires a different architecture or timetable. The answer depends on the freight task, not merely on the specifications of an available vessel.

The Dutch programme is a promising way to accelerate the first investments. Its next achievement should be to show that those investments connect: cargo moving on named routes, vessels using dependable shared energy infrastructure, and operating data strong enough to finance the following corridor without perpetually reinventing the subsidy. The ship is the visible part of the transition. The corridor is what makes it commercially useful.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing examines the subsidy rules, the Moerdijk battery-exchange arrangement and the difference between vessel awards and operational corridors. The open, 98-page Inland Freight Electrification report contains the modelled cargo, fleet, battery-pool, terminal and investment evidence behind that analysis.

Connecticut solar projects directly support Veterans Affairs campus

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Connecticut Department of Veterans Affairs (DVA), Connecticut Green Bank and their partners recently marked the completion of solar installations serving DVA’s main campus and skilled nursing facility in Rocky Hill, Connecticut.

The projects will deliver renewable electricity and reduce energy costs at the campus. The Green Bank developed the projects through its Solar Marketplace Assistance Program Plus (Solar MkAP+). Kearsarge Energy has partnered with Connecticut Green Bank as the long-term owners of these projects as well as nine others and will also oversee operations and maintenance. Greenskies Clean Energy was the engineering, procurement and construction contractor for the two DVA installations.

“To Gov. Lamont and your administration, thank you for having the vision for the GreenerGov CT initiative, providing reliable, environmentally friendly and cost-saving energy for residents and businesses in Connecticut, which is one of your top priorities, along with countless others, enabling Connecticut’s 3.6 million residents to thrive,” said Ron Welch, a retired brigadier general with the U.S. Army, and commissioner of the DVA. “Our team at the Connecticut Department of Veterans Affairs thanks you for your full support of this solar field project and for all you do through your significant partnerships to sustain and improve the environment and the quality of life for Connecticut residents and beyond.”

The two systems have a combined capacity of 1.404 MWDC and are projected to generate approximately 1.84 million kWh of electricity annually. The systems are projected to offset 85% of electricity use for the skilled nursing facility and 35% of the electricity use at the main campus building.

“This project brings together two important commitments: advancing sustainability in state government and supporting the people who have served our nation,” said Michelle Gilman, commissioner of the Department of Administrative Services. “DAS is proud to support this project and the broader Greener Gov CT partnership.”

The two arrays built at the Rocky Hill campus are 604.8 kWDC and 799.2 kWDC.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

Reviving an Enduring Emblem of Simplicity, Loll Designs Launches the Here and There Collection

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The best designs almost always come out of necessity and quiet observation. In the 1940s, legendary ecologist Aldo Leopold constructed a remarkably straightforward wooden bench so that he could sit outside his Wisconsin farmstead and contemplate nature’s primal force; the fundamental need for its conservation. The back-slanted seat was constructed from readily available dimensional lumber and almost immediately presented itself as an act of unpretentious, serendipitous genius: the quick assembly of a functional furnishing devised for a specific need but ultimately, one with universal potential. The particular brilliance of the design was its x-shaped self-bracing geometry, zero waste configuration, and uncomplicated assembly.

“Anyone that’s handy with a saw and some fasteners and lumber could easily build an Aldo bench,” says Eric Swanson, president of Duluth-based outdoor furniture brand Loll Designs. “That’s the beauty of it.” The Minnesota manufacturer recently teamed up with Michigan industrial design studio Argenta Park to turn the foundational artifact into the Here and There Collection. Named for Leopold’s belief that our appreciation for the natural world should balance between what’s nearby and farther afield, the cohesive and complementary suite of furnishings includes a dining chair, dining table, and of course, a bench.

This contemporary reinterpretation was twelve years in the making. For his outdoor wedding in 2014, Argenta Park designer Ricky Biddle was faced with the same practical challenge Leopold surmounted decades earlier: creating sturdy, durable, and comfortable outdoor seating. He didn’t want to rent generic, flimsy event furniture, so he spent his bachelor party assembling rudimentary Aldo benches. It was almost as if influential Italian designer Enzo Mari had sanctioned the task as one of his open-source “Autoprogettazione” self designs.

The pieces have remained a fixture of Biddle and his family’s everyday life ever since. This permanence prompted him to reconsider how its raw genius might be retooled using advanced manufacturing, elevated ergonomics, and year-round durability. He brought the idea to Argenta Park founder Churu Yun and the Loll Designs engineering team.

The best type of historical preservation is the kind that implements the latest technologies and refined strategies; doesn’t focus on creating replicas merely out of nostalgic reverence. “Not all historical designs lend themselves to being evolved into a modern-day furniture piece,” says Brian King, Loll Designs Brand and Marketing Director. “It’s more about being hyper selective in a design that has meaning, history, or a deep-rooted memory and identifying what’s most valuable.” Loll had done this many times before, most notably by bringing the ubiquitous Adirondack Chair up to contemporary standards.

While still carrying the original design’s understated silhouette, the re-edited and expanded collection carefully addressed the physical limitations of simple timber construction. Softly contoured surfaces replace flat planks, streamlined fastener assembly, and the use of 100% recyclable high-density polyethylene (HDPE)—available in Black, Charcoal Grey, Fog, and Sage—simplifies ongoing maintenance.

This calculus responds to changing demands: contemporary homeowners are increasingly looking for low-maintenance solutions that can withstand harsh seasonal shifts without sacrificing aesthetic refinement or tactile comfort. “With historical designs comes historical maintenance and poor ergonomics,” Swanson adds. “People want to enjoy their time outdoors sitting in their furniture more than working on it, which is why using alternative materials to traditional wood is a benefit; why a curved seat and backrest is so important; and why the right proportions—the height of the dining table—make all the difference.”

Leopold is widely considered the founder of wildlife ecology and his bench stands testament to that; allowing people to enjoy nature for as long as they want. The new Here and There Collection carries on that legacy.

Walnut, leather, stone, and brass shape a new collection for the home, debuting at High Point Market this fall

Barker Associates Architecture Office pairs preserved architectural details with saturated hues, custom woodwork, and contemporary art in a four-story Clinton Hill home for empty nesters

Etihad Airways Scraps All Airbus A380 Flights To North America: See Map Now

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Michael Leak | Shutterstock, Simple Flying

Etihad Airways is making significant changes to its global network. This includes one route being dropped, many others with fewer frequencies, and some notable aircraft changes. This includes the Airbus A380 being removed from Toronto, which was the only route to North America on which the type was deployed. Singapore will also no longer see the superjumbo.

The changes have materialized following the removal of Etihad's Chief Revenue and Commercial Officer, Arik De. For a better understanding of the situation, you can read the 500+ comments in this One Mile At A Time article. His tenure at Etihad saw the airline, whose hub is in Abu Dhabi, grow substantially, while introducing some highly notable and questionable new routes, including to Luxembourg and Calgary.

The Gulf airline's final double-decker service to Toronto, and also Canada and North America as a whole, will be in just over two months, on January 18, 2027. The 486-seat superjumbo currently runs daily with the following schedule, which is, of course, driven by connectivity via the UAE capital, especially to/from India.

EY21: 3:20 AM-8:50 AM (14 hours and 30 minutes)

EY22: 2:25 PM-12:20 PM+1 (12 hours and 55 minutes)

From January 19, the 371-seat A350-1000 will be deployed to Canada's busiest airport. The equipment switch does not just mean 24% fewer seats for sale per day. It also means that Toronto will no longer have The Residence or first class, while the number of business seats will drop by 37% from 70 to 44. Economy capacity will fall by 19%, with a loss of 78 seats.

Premium seats on the long route will be reduced from 17% of the total on the A380 to 11% on the A350. Given how utterly critical the lower-yielding transit traffic is to/from India, this move is perhaps very sensible. It also brings Toronto in line with Etihad's other routes to the US and Canada, all of which are also on the A350 or the Boeing 787-9. Nearly all of them do not have first class.

The A380 first appeared in Toronto in June 2025. It was the first time that Etihad had deployed the type to Canada. This was notable for various reasons. For example, it was redeployed from New York JFK, marking the first time that Toronto had two A380 operators with regular flights. Etihad joined Emirates, which has flown the type there since 2009. Etihad's arrival meant that Canada had two double-decker users for the first time since British Airways pulled out of Vancouver in 2022.

The double-decker lasted a bit longer on flights from Abu Dhabi to Singapore than it did to Toronto. The 486-seater first appeared in Lion City in February 2025. The type flew daily until early April 2026, when the 226-seat 787-9 replaced it. After the A380 briefly returned in June, the 787-9 continues to fly.

Abu Dhabi To Singapore; Local Times (December 15-21)

Singapore To Abu Dhabi; Local Times (December 15-21

The plan was for the superjumbo to return on December 15 and operate daily thereafter. The type will still return on that date, but will now only operate through January 10 to capture peak traffic for Christmastime and the New Year. From January 11, the so-called Dreamliner, to use the Boeing moniker, will return. Unsurprisingly, Singapore's schedule is largely about connectivity to/from Europe.

The exit of Etihad's A380s is part of wider double-decker changes at SIN. For example, Qatar Airways removed the type from Doha to Singapore in March 2026, which was when Qantas stopped using it from Melbourne. British Airways introduced the equipment from London Heathrow six months later. However, Emirates's offering is growing, and the airline will now mainly have three daily services to Changi on the A380 next year.

Could more new routes be announced? Find out more here!

On January 11, the A380 will no longer fly to Singapore, followed by Toronto a week later. From January 19 onward, Etihad's A380 network will, for now at least, consist of Bangkok (served during the winter until the end of March 2027; daily), London Heathrow (two daily), Paris CDG (returns at the end of March 2027; daily), and Tokyo Narita (returns at the end of March 2027; daily).

All the changes mentioned in this article come about a month after Etihad reactivated its last A380. Ch-aviation shows that the carrier now has nine superjumbos, while the tenth airframe was scrapped. It remains to be seen if various aircraft will now be grounded or if they will be deployed elsewhere. There are few places to fly the A380.

Speaking anonymously on November 6, an Etihad employee said that "it became an openly talked-about thing within Revenue and Commercial that the A380 was being moved to different locations [destinations] with absolutely nothing to do with strategy." Now that De has gone, the type will be removed from Toronto and Singapore. Will Bangkok or Tokyo be next? Only time will tell.

The Aircraft That Beat The A350 As Airbus' Best Value & Most Operationally Practical Incremental Upgrade

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Airlinephoto | Shutterstock, Simple Flying

All of the widebodies in production: the A330-900, Airbus A350 family, Boeing 787 family, and upcoming 777X family have the potential to offer airlines the best value for money. Which, in the real world, is highly contingent on missions, existing inventory, requirements, and a large range of other factors specific to individual airlines.

To say the A330-900 beats the A350 in value for money is narrowly true only in these cases where the stars align. And align they do, as the A330-900 has attracted an impressive 500+ orderbook, including some cases where the A350-900 may have been a viable alternative.

Some airlines (such as Delta) are replacing their A330ceo with a mixed fleet of A330-900s and A350s. Others (such as Qantas) are replacing them with A350s and 787s, while China Eastern is replacing its A330ceos with A330neos. Still others may go another way and partially replace them with long-range narrowbody A321XLRs on thinner routes. Here are some of the benefits of the A330-900 specifically (not the failed A330-800).

Airbus originally intended to produce the widebody A350 in three variants: the shortened A350-800, the standard A350-900, and the stretched A350-1000. Some airlines, such as Hawaiian Airlines, placed orders for the A350-800. However, Airbus then decided to cancel the A350-800 variant and upgrade the legacy A330ceo in two variants, the shorter A330-800 and stretched A330-900, collectively the A330neo.

Hawaiian switched its order to the A330-800. However, as time went on, it became clear that the A330-800 was unpopular, and unpopularity has a habit of breeding more unpopularity, with airlines getting cold feet about being left with a variant few other airlines wanted.

Ultimately, Hawaiian (and other airlines) canceled its orders for the A330-800 and purchased the Boeing 787-9, only to see its future parent, Alaska Airlines, snatch them away. The real story of the A330neo is not the dismal failure of the A330-800, but the success of the A330-900. As an upgrade to an existing aircraft, its development was much cheaper and faster than developing a clean-sheet aircraft.

By September 2026, Airbus had accumulated around 511 firm orders for the A330-900, with the remaining A330neo orders being the residual 13 A330-800 orders. It is somewhat ironic that this article is dedicated to the success of the A330-900 when the sister A330-800 is one of history's worst-selling airliners.

The A350 is not a failure in any way. It is one of the world's most successful twin-engine widebody aircraft families ever developed. To date, it has accumulated around 1,628 firm orders, around three times more than the A330neo (almost all of which are for the Airbus A330-900). However, the expectations of the two aircraft are different.

The A330neo comes with new wings, new engines (Rolls-Royce's new Trent 7000 engine), modernized avionics, and other improvements, but it remains, at its core, a 1990s airliner. Airbus elected to capitalize on the low-hanging fruit that two decades of technological development offered, and forego the marginal benefit of a clean-sheet design.

While the clean-sheet A350 (and rival 787) is substantively made up of advanced composites and titanium, allowing the aircraft to be lighter, more efficient, and have a higher cabin pressure, the A330neo remains a mostly aluminum aircraft. Airbus says the A330neo offers around a 25% improvement in fuel burn per seat compared with older-generation aircraft like the Boeing 767-300ER.

It also says the A330neo offers up to 7% lower operating costs per seat than the rival Boeing 787. Caution should be taken with this second claim, and it would heavily depend on assumptions, missions, and other specific factors. Boeing likely runs its own calculations with a different set of assumptions and comes up with an inverse conclusion.

One advantage the A330neo boasts is that it lacks the high development costs of a clean-sheet design. For airlines, the trade-off is that the aircraft may be a bit less fuel-efficient than it could otherwise be, but also somewhat cheaper to purchase or lease. This is a trade-off that has been judged worthwhile by many airlines, although list prices of aircraft can be misleading given that airlines tend to receive discounts, especially in bulk.

It is better to focus on market values and lease rates: these are what airlines actually spend on their aircraft, rather than the fancy figures that manufacturers put in their brochures. A recent analysis by Acumen shows the five-year market value of Boeing 787-9s, Boeing 787-10s, A350-1000s, and A350-900s clustering around $120 million to $130 million.

The market value of the Boeing 777-300ER is lower at around $110 million, possibly on account of its relatively poor fuel economy relative to the previous aircraft. Meanwhile, the shortened 787-8 sits at just under $100 million.

The A330-900 is priced at around $87 million, making it considerably cheaper in capital terms. According to the 2026 ICLG Aviation Finance & Leasing report published in June 2026, a new A330-900 costs around $118.5 million. This is much less than the A350-900 at $168 million, the A350-1000 at $181 million, the Boeing 787-9 at $160 million, and the Boeing 787-10 at $168 million.

Many airlines lease their aircraft, and these rates largely mirror the current market value. For factory-new aircraft, the going lease rate for the A330-900 is within the range of $902,000 to $975,000 per month. Meanwhile, its A350-900 counterpart is around $1.19 million to $1.25 million a month, and the stretched A350-1000 is higher still.

When it comes to the relevant Dreamliners for this comparison, the Boeing 787-9 is being leased at around $1.07 million to $1.15 million a month, and the stretched 787-10 is generating $1.13 million to $1.23 million a month in leasing revenue. Naturally, the market values and lease rates for legacy Boeing 777s and Airbus A330-200s and A330-300s are much lower.

However, these aircraft are much less fuel-efficient, and their maintenance expenses rise as they age. The quad-engined A340-300 and A340-600 are also no longer relevant as a point of comparison for the A330-900, as the A340 family has almost completely retired as scheduled passenger aircraft in mainline airlines.

A few A340-300s remain to be phased out, while Lufthansa has finally withdrawn the last of its A340-600s. Some of these old A340s are finding an epilogue in sanctioned countries like Iran, but this is irrelevant to where market forces are operating freely.

One of the most important things airlines look for when structuring their fleets is commonality. Maintenance is one of the leading expenses for maintaining aircraft and commonality is one of the main ways to reduce the expense. Extreme examples of airlines pursuing commonality are Southwest and Ryanair (only Boeing 737s) and IndiGo (A320s).

The older bones of the A330neo are a disadvantage in fuel efficiency, as discussed above, but it does come with an upside beyond basic cost. The A330neo shares around 95% of its airframe spare parts with older A330ceos. This allows the almost 150 airlines already flying the A330ceo to add or switch to the A330neo with minimal training and lower marginal maintenance burden.

Notably, pilots going from the ceo to neo only need half a day of computer-based self-study. This commonality can save airlines around $6 million per aircraft in maintenance, although exact savings will vary considerably. The A330neo also boasts commonality with the clean-sheet A350, even if it's less than with its A330ceo predecessor.

This is relevant for airlines like Delta, which operate both the A330 and A350. Naturally, all this means the A330neo faces an uphill battle convincing airlines already operating Boeing aircraft to switch. The argument inverts.

Paradoxically, one of the strengths of the A330neo is precisely that the A350 and Boeing 787 are successful and popular. The massive popularity of those two aircraft has created massive backorders, with Airbus and Boeing struggling to ramp up production. An airline placing a new order today may have to go to the back of the queue and expect to get the aircraft sometime in the mid 2030s.

Still, the three-times greater A350 order book is muted by the fact that its production rate is higher. Assuming a new order goes to the back of the queue and using Airbus' September 2026 production guidance, new orders for A350-900s will be delivered in 6–8 years (2032-2034). The A330-900 ordered today may be delivered around a year earlier (2031-2034). Planned production rates are 12 for the flagship A350 and five for the A330neo.

Found an error? Send it info@simpleflying.com so it can be corrected.

The elephant in the room in this conversation about the strength of the A330neo relative to the A350 (and 787) is that they are different aircraft. The A330-900 is not a direct one-for-one equivalent to the A350-900. Forgetting costs and fuel efficiency, they are different aircraft, with different capacities and ranges, and have been optimized for different missions. There is overlap, but that only goes so far.

JetBlue's CEO has pushed back on creditor fears and is betting on premium to return to profitability

📅 2026-11-11  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

JetBlue Airways executives have sought to calm investors after shares in the airline hit a record low following a report that bondholders had contracted restructuring advisors. "We have no intention, no plans to file for bankruptcy. We are not engaged with any restructuring company," CEO Joanna Geraghty told CNBC in an interview last month.

Shares have bounced back and are up 40% so far this month as of Tuesday's close.

Geraghty has said the company is focused on its turnaround plan. A major part of that strategy is the airline betting on the premium travel boom to make it profitable again. It will start the next chapter of the effort on Wednesday, when it unveils its new domestic first-class seats at its home hub at John F. Kennedy International Airport.

Tune in at 10:50 a.m. ET as JetBlue CEO Joanna Geraghty joins CNBC TV to discuss the company's target of getting back to profitability. Watch in real time on CNBC+ or the CNBC Pro stream.

JetBlue announced it would offer a domestic first class — BlueFirst — in December 2024 and plans to start flying aircraft with the new seats and full on-board product next month. Retrofits will continue during 2027, Geraghty told CNBC late last month.

BlueFirst is different from the lie-flat, longer-haul business class, Mint, that JetBlue offers on transcontinental and Europe routes. The carrier plans to offer BlueFirst on flights on popular leisure routes throughout Florida and the Caribbean.

The New York airline last posted a profit in 2019, before the pandemic. JetBlue is betting that resilient travel demand, especially for roomier seats, will help fuel its turnaround plan, which aims to get the carrier to an operating profit next year and $1 in adjusted earnings per share in 2028.

The company reported it ended the third quarter with $2.2 billion in liquidity and that its unencumbered asset base is now $7 billion, including $2.8 billion in aircraft and engines.

JetBlue had total debt of $8.4 billion as of Sept. 30, the carrier said in its earnings filing.

Geraghty told CNBC last month there is a "fundamental disconnect between the narrative in the bond market and what our financial results actually demonstrate."

When asked last month about whether JetBlue's eye on keeping costs down includes furloughs, layoffs or buyouts, Geraghty said "absolutely not."

JetBlue shares are still down 12% so far this year. Deutsche Bank upgraded the airline last week, citing airlines' conservative growth and a focus on yields versus filling planes.

Airlines large and small are racing to outfit their cabins with roomier, more expensive seating options to help cover ever-higher costs, from jet fuel to labor to airport operation fees.

For example, a ticket in the new JetBlue recliner first-class seats between Kennedy Airport and Fort Lauderdale-Hollywood International Airport in Florida is going for $847 roundtrip in the first week of March, while standard economy is $446.

On Tuesday, budget carrier Frontier Airlines also unveiled its new first class seats, which will take up the first two rows, and said it would outfit its Airbus fleet with premium seats that have extra legroom and in-seat power through row eight. The airline will take out a row of seating on its planes to make the pricier ones fit, Chief Marketing Officer Bobby Schroeter told CNBC in an interview.

Hanifa Is Relaunching After an 8-Month Hiatus

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Hanifa is back. A little over eight months after founder Anifa Mvuemba paused operations for her label, which had been in business for 15 years, it’s relaunching with a new collection and updated business model.

The collection features the colorful party clothes the brand is known for, like a red evening gown with a bright blue feathered brooch and a blue liquid organza dress (a 2022 revival bound to become the season’s made-to-order showstopper). There are also more practical pieces, like Hanifa’s take on the track pant (barrel-legged with a silk stripe) and bomber jacket. The Harper dress, a green and black-dotted hit from last year, is now available in a deep blue.

Mvuemba hopes the new Hanifa feels true to the brand’s original ethos, just a little more mature: the designer was 21 when she started the brand in 2011, and now that she’s about to turn 36, her style and taste has evolved, she says, alongside her customers’. Mvuemba spent months reworking the brand’s fabrications, cuts and patterns, especially for knitwear, its biggest category. She went through dozens of iterations to find the right cashmere blend, for example, that will hold its shape and have cashmere’s soft handfeel, while keeping the brand in the price range its customers have come to expect.

The brand worked to improve its fabric quality without compromising on price.

The relaunch will be a test in regaining consumer trust. Things began to unwind last December, when purchases made during her brand’s annual Hanifa Friday sale in November failed to ship, thanks to production delays and supply chain issues. For some customers, months went by without any communication — or delivery — from the brand. Disgruntled customers took to social media to complain, not just about delayed orders (all of which Hanifa said it was eventually able to fulfill, or issued refunds for), but about the brand’s general direction, which they believed went downhill. In a statement posted online in March, Mvuemba apologized, and announced the brand would go on hiatus.

“Mentally, I wasn’t prepared to continue,” Mvuemba says. “I wasn’t in the right headspace. And in order for me to be the best version of myself, the best leader, mother, wife, I could be to my family, my team, my business — I needed that mental clarity to just take a step back, rest, recalibrate, to get a clear view on what we needed to do for the business as a whole. I needed a break,” Mvuemba says. “I’m just not the type of founder or person that performs for the optics. It was extremely hard to do, but it was necessary, because in this space, it’s not something that we see often. But it’s something that we as founders need to be able to do.”

Hanifa’s new collection comes after the brand paused orders in March.

In the end, Mvuemba only took about two weeks off from work, she says. She found a therapist and spent time with her baby, who was born in December and whose maternity care was interrupted by crisis management in the fallout. Then, she turned her attention to Hanifa 2.0.

“I love this business so much. I love this brand so much. I had to get back,” she says. “We uprooted everything. We looked at our communications, our branding, our sizing, our website. Now, everything — our processes, our manufacturers — has shifted, and it’s nice to have had this time to sit through everything, go back, revise, go back, revise, and then say, ‘OK, we’re ready.’”

Now the brand is ready to relaunch, Hanifa is returning to market on different terms.

Mvuemba is eyeing outerwear as an expansion opportunity for her collection.

The biggest change to Hanifa’s operations is switching to a ready-to-ship model, versus a past pre-order model. With no pre-order period, where the brand could produce only a committed number of SKUs, the company has fronted the entire new collection (which Mvuemba says is “expensive but necessary”) and plans to use real-time performance data to determine production cadence and inventory investments. Having inventory overhead also shortens lead times for purchases to be fulfilled, and gives the brand space to ship items by sea rather than air, which cuts costs.

“We thought it was really important to come out the door swinging,” Mvuemba says. “People want their things when they want their things.” It’s also invested in new customer service and logistics partners, with brand plans to build data analysis into its production cycle to ensure that it’s responding quicker to what’s selling. Mvuemba hired a brand strategist, an art director and a PR firm to help steer the comeback story. The brand has always been bootstrapped, but she says she’s open to outside funding if the right partner comes along. She’s also open to the right retail partners, but that right now, the direct-to-consumer model is the best fit thanks to production and shipping timing.

Her focus above all, she says, is her customer. Mvuemba comes back time and again to the importance of data, and how much she wants to spend the rollout of this collection listening to feedback. She hopes that the brand’s core customer — the company has a legion of longtime loyalists who were sad to see it go — returns, and that the brand can grow its reach. By category, she’s excited about expanding the brand’s shoes and outerwear, both of which are steps in building a more complete ready-to-wear label, which is her main goal for next year. Scaling, Mvuemba says, will come at the right pace.

“I used to say I want it to be as big as LVMH. And with this year, I just had so much time to reflect and I’m OK with the pace that we’re going,” she says. “I just want to have a globally recognized brand that a lot of women can identify with and that we can continue to create these beautiful, amazing pieces. I’m so excited to show them this new era of the brand. We’re sharper, we’re clearer, we understand who our girl is, who our customer is, and I’m excited to give them more.”

Stove God Cooks & Swizz Beatz Talk Making ‘All This For Me?’: ‘Album of the Decade’

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The duo's long-awaited and much-talked-about collab album has found a home — and they're excited for fans to hear what they've put together.

Stove God Cooks is finally ready to follow up his underground classic Reasonable Drought, which he dropped alongside Roc Marciano back in 2020. Since then, he’s been on an impressive feature run (mainly on Grisleda records) and has had to deal with a plethora of leaks that have only sent demand for a sophomore album to a fever pitch, especially after his scene-stealing chorus on the Clipse‘s LET GOD SORT EM OUT standout “F.I.C.O.” Related Hilary Duff Reflects On Her Massive Lucky Me Tour: 'I'm Really Proud of Myself, and That's Hard to Feel As An Adult' Sade Won't Be Attending the Rock Hall Induction Ceremony The Chicks & Kacey Musgraves Complete Historic All-Female State Fayre 2027 Headline Bill Explore Stove God Cooks Swizz Beatz See latest videos, charts and news

Now, after a year of label drama and false starts — including one scrapped project in Goat Stamp, and a March 27 release date for the album we’re here to talk about — his parent label Babygrande has a entered a joint partnership with Warner Records in an effort to finally give fans what they’ve been waiting for in a new full-length album.

Part of the reason for the delay has been because Stove wasn’t really aware of how things worked on the sample-clearing side of the business — because he’s used to working alongside prolific rappers like Griselda Records founder Westside Gunn, who craft albums and drop them in a span of a week.

“I didn’t understand a lot of s—t, you know? So, things that was pissing me off, it was really the way I was seeing s—t,” he explains to me over Zoom. “My references wasn’t the way s—t really go. I’m seeing my people move this way. Why we taking so long to drop albums? I see West drop an album in three days. I know he got samples in his s—t. I’m on the songs. So, it’s that kind of s—t. But once you rip the sheet off and Gunn starts telling me about the lawsuits and s—t that he deals with, I’m like, ‘Oh, no, this s—t is for real.'”

Now, alongside legendary producer Swizz Beatz, he’s finally beginning the rollout for his long-awaited sophomore album All This For Me? They first dropped the warm up single, “Welcome to My Garden,” earlier this year in February (which has been “elevated,” according to Stove and Swizz) and the duo recently released the project’s second single and music video in “Stovie’s Home (Down Down)” this past Friday (Nov. 6). And the Syracuse, N.Y. rapper is excited for his fans to finally hear what he’s been working on and put the rollercoaster that was this past year behind him.

“The s—t is a super blessing, man,” he says. “That’s the reality of this moment for me right now. All the other s—t, the old s—t, none of s—t matter to me right now.”

Check out my conversation with Stove God Cooks & Swizz Beatz below as we talk their creative process, their chemistry and what fans can expect once they finally get to hear what they’ve been cooking up.

So, the word is you signed a deal with Warner. So what can you tell us about that?

You’ve ask Jay-Z for help in regards to your label situation, did he play a part in any of this? Whether that be with the Warner partnership or you and Swizz linking up.

Stove God Cooks: Definitely me and Swizz. Hov planted the seed, but he didn’t have anything to do with Warner.

Swizz Beatz: I always show Hov a lot of the artists that I’m working with, and I was working on — I think Godfather of Harlem season one — and I was just showing him all of these different vibes, and he was just like, “Yo, you and Stove gotta get together immediately. He’s one of my favorites out right now. He got it. And you can compliment his sound. He can compliment your sound, You need to get on top of that.”

And I take what he says serious — because Hov has got great insight, and I love to see him listening to artists like Stove. That’s what he’s playing in the crib, smoking a cigar, vibing — and I just thought that was super cool. Now we here and people are gonna get to see the results. I was already a fan, but Hov definitely put the stamp on making it happen.

I just like the way it organically happened. Because even beyond Hov saying that, Stove was really, very talented, and everything that I thought he was already, even more. So, that was just a blessing. I’m just happy to be a part of the project and just riding it out for him until all of the business got settled. Most people would have just left, but once we connected, it was just like, “Yo, I’m already committed.” So, whatever we need to do to make it happen, the world need to hear this talent, this energy, this sound. It’s just refreshing. I’m happy to be a part of it.

How did the album come together? Did you already have a pack of beats with Stove in mind?

Swizz Beatz: The first, initial vibe of us was stuff I had, but once we started getting into the conversations and the ideas, and me speaking to him and actually knowing him, everything was tailor made after that. Myself and Avenue Beatz, we just wanted to put together the best s—t for him that would complement his style.

And the crazy part is, the album took a little bit long, but even the verses got better. You know what I’m saying? The verses got better, the the beats got better. Everything got better with this little wait period that we had. We used the best out of it and kept elevating the project. Stove would be like, “Yo, wait till you hear the new verse.” I’m like, “Damn.” [Laughs.]

Swizz Beatz: He’s different, man. They ass is grass.

Stove God Cooks: The first initial pack he sent, I was like, “Oh, I gotta show him I’m for real.” So I was like, “Yeah, let me lock in” I sent a few back, like five.

You guys are dropping another single with “Stovie’s Home,” but a while back you released “Welcome to My Garden.” Is that still going to be on the album or is that just like a loosie now?

Swizz Beatz: And it’s elevated, by the way.

Swizz Beatz: We wasn’t really prepared as we thought we was when we dropped that. There were still things that needed to be handled. It was like a start and a stop situation. People love that joint, but the cool part is, I think they’re gonna love it even better now.

You added some bells and whistles in post production.

Swizz Beatz: Bells, whistles, hammers. Saws and s—t.

Were you guys able to link up together in the studio, or was this done over email and text? What was the process in putting this together?

Stove God Cooks: A little bit of both, but the energy is always there.

And you guys went back to the lab, though, and made new records. Is what you’re saying too?

Stove God Cooks: Yeah, we did that too. After the little slowdown period, when we had to figure some things out, we locked back in and made it that Frankenstein moment.

Is there any particular reason why you guys are waiting to drop this early next year?

Swizz Beatz: We’re not really waiting on anything, which is why the song comes out Friday. When it comes out, we already starting. The project deserved the runway. And with the visuals and the storytelling, people won’t even feel that because we gonna be hitting them with so much action, bro. They gonna know what time it is.

I think that dropping the project all at one time, I think it sounds great — but I think that giving people the story that’s leading to it, I think Stove deserved that, because it took so long to even get here for him to just throw out the project without any storytelling. I think the storytelling is great. I think that people are gonna have so much content that they’re gonna appreciate the album more. Give people what they actually been waiting for.

Stove God Cooks: That’s what it deserves.

Swizz Beatz: Yeah and there’s not going to be silence at all. Once we pop this thing off, it’s go time. It ain’t no more stopping. We got so many plans. They ass is grass.

You said that in the trailer. I’m assuming you guys shot the video already too?

Swizz Beatz: They ass is grass. [Laughs.] I’m tellin’ you.

You initially were gonna drop one album that you were happy with, and you scrapped it, then Swiss sent you some stuff and it got you back motivated. What was it about Swizz’s production that got you excited?

Stove God Cooks: Just the energy. It felt like what we do with with jumper cables on it. It felt like the soulful s—t like the Griselda lane and everything that I’m coming out of times a million. Just with the drums and Swizz’s touch on in our world. That’s what it felt like.

Swizz, what is it about artists like Stove that gets you excited. Because you’ve been in the game for a minute. You’ve done it all, and seen it all. How does he motivate you?

Swizz Beatz: I just love that he really, really, really takes his craft seriously. Stove is a dangerous weapon. It’s like a samurai sword that’s getting sharpened in Japan by a family of masters that have been around for hundreds of years. They make a blade cut like butter, and it could also go through a brick.

Like the Hattori Hanzo sword in Kill Bill.

Swizz Beatz: Yeah, he’s that. I like that he show people better than he could tell you because Stove don’t really speak like that. He speaks with his art, with his craft. A lot of people talk so much and their craft don’t match up to it. I love that Stove speaks through his craft, his art. He could be going through a lot of s—t and he be quiet, but that music is gonna rumble in the jungle, and I just I’m just used to that. Because that’s like Jadakiss. That’s how Kiss is. That’s how Styles is. They like Ginsu knives. They hit you with some s—t, and it’s like, “Damn, that’s what you’ve been thinking this whole time?”

I came up around nothing but lyricists, with the Ruff Ryders and the other artists I was producing for, when you really had to be who you say you was for people to feel you. Stove belongs in that category. It’s like the no-gimmicks category. It’s great to be able to work with artists like that in ’26, when I started in ’98.

That’s a perfect segue, because you came up with an underground imprint with the Ruff Ryders and you guys were able to break through into the mainstream. I think Stove is in a similar position. How can you use your experience to help him do the same?

Swizz Beatz: I mean, he’s doing most of the work. We’re just supporting. And that’s the same thing with The LOX and DMX and everybody. They did the work. They just needed the supporting team. That’s why I like this partnership with Warner. I think it’s a positive addition to what he’s doing, and even for Babygrande to understand that it makes sense. I think that’s a power move for everybody, because the more that we can get behind the his talent, the more he gonna fly, and people want it.

We put up a BTS, and there’s more comments on that joint than a person that got 100 million followers. So, the engagement is there, the people, the audience is there. He got a product that people want. We just gotta give him the right airplane to take off and land with, and the rest is history. He’s very selective of what he does, which I think is great, and I think the consistency from this point on is going to be major for him and for us. I think this is a great celebration. The energy’s right, the visuals are right, these interviews are right. I think that it’s worth the wait.

A lot of people want to hang on to the negative side of what they’ve seen. Now we gotta celebrate the positive side. Even like with us posting stuff, I’m correcting people and I really don’t usually do that, but you got to set that tone. Like, yo, we not on that timing, we not talking about yesterday, we giving you what you want today. Let’s stop talking about yesterday because today’s a new day for him and for the project, and for the projects, you know what I’m sayin’? We ready to rock and roll. I’m with whatever.

Are you guys working on multiple projects? Is what you’re saying?

Stove, I’m assuming you have a bunch of projects in the stash because you basically try to every day.

Stove God Cooks: Absolutely, but I’m not just recording just to record. The s—t that Swizz was sending me the first time was so inspiring. That’s what “Garden” came out of, and a couple other joints that made that album. When I’m inspired its nonstop, but I never want to get to the point where I’m just doing it just to do it. It’s when I got something to say. There’s joints that I recorded that Swizz heard and was like, “Nah, bro. I understand you going through this, but nah, bro.” I was talking too crazy.

It’s all inspiration. Whether it’s, “I’m going through something,” or, “We’re gonna step on everybody.”

You mean Swizz was telling you to be a little bit more political with what you say?

Stove God Cooks: Nah, not at all. He’s not telling me anything. He’s just saying…

Swizz Beatz: I can’t tell that man what to say.

Stove God Cooks: With the joints I sent, I was just like in my feelings. It’s never like “Nah, we can’t do this.” It’s more like, “You sure?”

Okay, I was just trying to clarify that. Is it going to be easier for you to put out music in this new situation? Will you be going on hiatus again after dropping this project with Swizz?

Stove God Cooks: We don’t know what what it’s gonna be. You’re thinking too far ahead.

You’ve been active, though. You’re on that song with Fat Joe and Jadakiss, and then you’re on another one with French Montana and Max B. You’re kind of the go-to hook guy right now. “F.I.C.O.” helped put you on another level. How did you end up on those tracks?

Stove God Cooks: That Fat Joe joint came from me working with Dre. When Joe started working on his album, Dre was like, “Yo, I played that joint for Joe, and he wanted [it].” So I said, “Yeah, give it to him.” I just had a hook on there. I didn’t have a verse yet.

You mentioned elsewhere that you had played the French song for Gunn, and he told you it wasn’t the right time. So, when is the right time to drop something? Have you figured that out?

Stove God Cooks: Well, he said that record wasn’t the right record for me. That’s why I ended up giving it back to French. He said, “After all this time, that can’t be your record.” But when “Garden” came out, he said, “Now this is what your record’s supposed to sound like.”

Stove God Cooks: “Then he said, “Send me the beat.”

Oh, okay, so he’s gonna be on the remix or is he going to be on…

Stove God Cooks: I told you we elevated it.

Is there anything else you guys can tell us about the album that’s not gonna spoil anything?

Stove God Cooks: It’s amazing, man. I listened to it this morning while I was jogging. I’m like, “This n—a’s amazing.”

How many miles you put in listening to that?

Stove God Cooks: Oh, I do like five miles a day.

I wanted to ask you about what good with you and Gunn too. Are we still getting Michelle Records?

Stove God Cooks: Yeah, the Michelle album is coming also, but he got Heelmatic dropping this. I’m on that. That’s a dope moment. But right now, Stovie’s home. I gotta accept that this my moment, and really cement my footprint right now. Etch my name in the wall of the pyramid for real. We did a lot already, but this is the moment of of cementing everything.

How do you feel now? I feel like this past year has kinda been a headache for you.

Stove God Cooks: Nah, I’m good. S—t happens, but I done seen worse s—t. We’re making amazing music, I got a lot of money. I can’t complain for real. I get upset because my fans deserve the music, but I rather them get it this way, rolled out on a red carpet with a real runway. The partnership is dope. They done dropped the bag. We good. I don’t have no reason to be mad.

Did you get in trouble for saying you sent Jay a hook. There was a lot of chatter online. Did you get the “This is HOV” text?

Stove God Cooks: Bro, I’m grown as f—k. What the f—k are you talking about? [Laughs.]

Jay’s been known to be secretive, so the talk online was about you maybe ruining an opportunity for letting the cat out of the bag.

Stove God Cooks: I’m just one million percent honest.

Now that he announced the Super Bowl, fans feel like an album might come right before or right after.

Stove God Cooks: Even if he didn’t have that chatter around him about doing an album or whatever, I still would’ve said that I sent him a hook.

Swizz Beatz: I don’t feel no album coming out. You crazy? Ain’t no album coming out.

Stove God Cooks: It had nothing to do with me confirming there was an album. I don’t think there’s an album, but I still sent the hook. And like I said on there, “No matter what you use it for, just use it, big homie.”

Swizz, your theory is that there’s no album coming out?

Swizz Beatz: I would have been working on it.

Listen, you could still be working on it, but it’s cool.

Swizz Beatz: But who knows, that man a wizard.

Swizz Beatz: He’s very unpredictable. But I speak to him a lot and I just haven’t felt that motion.

How many tracks can fans expect to hear on All This for Me?

Stove God Cooks: Thirteen. Album of the decade.

Okay, and we got one confirmed feature with Westside Gunn being on “Welcome to My Garden.” We”ll have to wait and see for the rest of the features.

Stove God Cooks: Yeah, West is on there. The album is amazing, bro. I’m not gonna hold you. Not just because it’s me listening to it. Sometimes I gotta step out of myself and listen to it. The album is amazing for real. From the production to me being able to say what I wanted to say and really being able to get off my feelings. And it still has that dusty, raw feeling, I’m still talking the way I talk.

And the album is finished? You guys are done tinkering with it?

Stove God Cooks: Yeah, the album is done.

Stove God Cooks: Yeah, bro. You asking with a lot of doubt.

I’m just asking questions, bro. [Laughs.]

Swizz Beatz: Yo, Stove, I ain’t gonna lie, y’all got people traumatized over there.

Stove God Cooks: Nah, for real. That’s that’s how he asking the question.

Listen, this is my third interview with Stove this year, man.

Stove God Cooks: Seriously, and with every one the energy is different.

It’s not that I don’t believe you, I’m just trying to get more details about the album, since its not dropping just yet.

Stove God Cooks: I’ma keep it real, man. It’s a blessing, even to partner with Warner. We were doing the album either way, but we were able to do that partnership and we got a lot of money out of that s—t. The album is amazing, and to have this opportunity to work with the Big Homie and him really putting the jumper cables on my s—t and take it to where I always felt it should be.

Right now, it’s just getting the fans what they want, and I’m standing on this album. This s—t is amazing. The Most High got a funny way of leading me where I gotta get to. It don’t make sense, not even to me, until we get there, and then it’s like, “Oh, this was worth the wait.” Because what else can you do? Just let it play out.

All the talking stops at some point. All the faking stops at some point. All the hating stops at some point. All these broke n—as, they power get shut off at some point, so they can’t charge them bum ass phones no more and comment. All this s—t stop at some point, and then everything just got to play out the way it’s supposed to. And that’s where I’m at.
