Music Industry Moves: Shore Fire Names Jaclyn D. Childress President; Epic Records Promotes Ayanna Wilks and Tina Ibañez; Jack Antonoff Names Date for Ally Coalition Benefit Concert

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Top communications firm Shore Fire Media has announced the promotion of Jaclyn D. Childress to president. A 13-year veteran of the firm and member of its leadership team since 2019, Childress (pictured above, left) will continue to head Shore Fire’s Nashville team, reporting to recently appointed CEO Angela Barkan, with a focus on sports, country music, reality TV and creators.

Hired as a publicity coordinator in 2013, Childress rose through the company’s publicist ranks to become a vice president in 2021, then growing to senior vice president three years later. Related Stories Tom Hanks Returning to Broadway After 14 Years for New Play 'This World of Tomorrow' Pamela Abdy Delivers Fiery Speech After Warner Bros. Exit: ‘It Is Getting Harder, Not Easier, to Be a Woman in the Business’

“In our short time working together, Jaclyn’s entrepreneurial nature has become very apparent,” said Barkan. “Besides overseeing our Nashville office, which works with prominent music businesses and talent, she signed our first creator in 2019 and has established our thriving sports practice. I know she will continue to help build the business as an integral part of our executive leadership team.”

+ Epic Records has promoted Ayanna Wilks to Senior Vice President, Head of Publicity, and Tina Ibañez to Senior Vice President, Creative & Content, effective immediately, the company annoucned. Both will report to Ezekiel Lewis, Chairman and CEO of Epic Records.

Wilks (pictured above, center) will lead Epic’s publicity department from Los Angeles. She joined Epic in 2019 as Senior Director of Publicity and rose to Vice President before stepping into her new role. Previously, she held roles with Wilhelmina Models, Roc Nation and Diane von Furstenberg.

In her new role, Ibañez (pictured above, right) will lead Epic’s creative department from New York. She joined Epic in 2021, where she has shaped the visual worlds of artists across the roster.

Ibañez was previously a Senior Art Director at Sony Music, where she earned a Grammy Award with fellow creative Brett Kilroe. She began her career at design studio Trooper.

“Great artists need partners who can tell their story and build their world. Ayanna and Tina do both at the highest level. Ayanna understands how to shape an artist’s narrative and connect it to culture, and Tina has a rare gift for turning an artist’s vision into something you can see and feel. Together, they’ve helped define what Epic stands for. As we invest in the next generation of Epic talent, they’re exactly the leaders I want building it with us,” Lewis said.

+ The Ally Coalition has announced its 12th Annual Talent Show will take place Tuesday, December 15 at the Jack H. Skirball Center for the Performing Arts in New York City, at 7:30pm. Tickets will be open for pre-sale this Wednesday, November 11 and to the general public Thursday, November 12 at 10am ET.

Created and curated by TAC Founder, Jack Antonoff, the annual Talent Show has raised more than $2 million to support unhoused LGBTQ+ youths and has featured performances by Taylor Swift, Lana Del Rey, Sabrina Carpenter, Rachel Zegler, Phoebe Bridgers, Hayley Williams, Hasan Minhaj, Kasey Musgraves, Florence Welch, Jason Isbell, Clairo, and St. Vincent, among dozens of others.

“The Talent Show exists in its own special world that we have built over the last eleven years: bringing together my favorite artists to play a small room in New York for our people,” notes Jack Antonoff. “All proceeds go to The Ally Coalition and our work supporting the homeless LGBTQ+ community – means the world to me and everyone who works hard to make this night happen every year.”

+ The Recording Academy has promoted Joanna Chu to SVP of Awards, Susan Stewart to VP of the Songwriters & Composers Wing, and Michael Nieporent has been named SVP of Global Partnerships & Business Development.

Chu previously served as VP of Awards for five years, leading the department through all aspects of Grammy season and reinforcing key Academy initiatives throughout the Awards process. Stewart has led the S&C Wing since she helped establish it in 2020 — first announced on the 63rd Grammy Awards Telecast — and has grown it to represent nearly half of the Academy’s voting membership. Nieporent joins the Academy with more than 17 years of experience building and leading partnerships across entertainment, media and live events, including the Academy of Country Music Awards, the American Music Awards, and the Billboard Music Awards.

+ Sony Music Publishing U.K. has signed songwriter-artist Natalie Findlay, which includes all future works. Her recent work includes co-written songs with Suki Waterhouse (“Good Looking”), Ashe (“Swan Lake”), joining a discography that includes releases from Remy Bond, Blossoms and Declan McKenna.

Findlay said, “I’m really happy to be joining Sony Music Publishing for my next chapter. I’ve always been drawn to artists with something unique to say and to create music that feels real and exciting. I’m lucky to have a new team around me who understands that and shares my enthusiasm for what’s ahead.”

+ Prescription Songs has signed Phil Leigh, a Los Angeles–based songwriter-producer whose credits include Le Sserafim’s “Spaghetti,” Twice’s “Hi Hello,” ANOTR’s “Paradise,” and Jason Derulo and Michael Bublé’s “Spicy Margarita.” He has also collaborated on songs by Beyoncé, David Guetta, the Chainsmokers and J Balvin, with forthcoming releases from Marshmello, ANOTR, and Purple Disco Machine.

Leigh said, “Life is the best! And a HUGE part of why I feel that way is because of music. I’m truly obsessed with it and I don’t take for granted that I’ve been allowed to spend my life feeling, playing and creating professionally. I’ve been close with the Prescription Songs family for a very long time and it means the world to me that I’m now officially a part of it, working with them. They match my freak, and I’m so grateful that they are the people that I’m doing this with.”

+ TuneCore has named Tash Shah as the company’s new Vice President, Global Marketing. She will report to Chief Business Officer Brian Miller. In her role, she will lead worldwide marketing strategy, spanning brand and creative, customer acquisition, lifecycle and retention, and product marketing.

Shah joined TuneCore in 2023 as Vice President, International, where she led international strategy, held P&L responsibility for each market, and managed teams across 17 countries. Prior to joining TuneCore, she spent 12 years at Spotify, where she served as the company’s first Head of Marketing in the UK, enacting some of the platform’s earliest in-house Artist Marketing campaigns and securing partnerships with the BBC, Nike, and more.

Miller commented, “Independent artists who are already building an audience need more than a place to upload music. They need a partner that helps them grow. Tash has spent three years understanding what those artists need, and she has the vision to make TuneCore the platform they choose to build their careers on. I’m excited to see her lead our global marketing team into this next chapter.”

+ Anuel AA collaborator La Greña has signed with Vydia in conjunction with ShowOff Gang Music for the upcoming release of his album “Diferente,” due on Nov. 27. La Greña will drop the forthcoming project’s first single “Todos Loco,” on Friday.

Speaking on the signing, ShowOff Gang Music President Jason “JT” Thomas said, “La Greña represents the kind of raw talent, authenticity, and star power we’re building ShowOff Gang Music around. Partnering with Vydia to introduce him to the world is more than just a strategy; it’s a statement that we believe in his vision, his sound, and his ability to make a global impact. We’re not just signing an artist; we’re investing in the next major voice coming out of the Dominican Republic.”

Diplo and Former Downtown Music CEO Andrew Bergman Launch Indie Publisher Bison Music

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The company is focused on original music and long-term artist development.

Diplo and Andrew Bergman, the former CEO of Downtown Music, are launching Bison Music, an independent music publisher focused on signing and developing songwriters early in their careers.

Bergman will serve as the company’s CEO, working alongside COO Tom Lee, CCO Juba Lee and general manager Jenny Ilibassi. The company’s roster of songwriters includes Picard Bros., Leclair, Maesic, Nitti, Kaien Cruz, BomboCat, Akira Evans and Groupthink, a group whose credits span songs for artists including BTS, Dua Lipa, Bad Bunny and JENNIE.

Bison is positioning itself in contrast to a current publishing landscape that’s has been dominated by catalog acquisition. Rather than buying existing intellectual property, the company says its focused on long-term artist development and “making fresh, original music written by real people to connect with a global audience.”

“For me, there’s still nothing more exciting in music than discovering a great song from someone you’ve never heard of,” Bergman says in a press release. “That’s always been at the core of what makes music publishing a great business. And it’s what excited me about partnering with Diplo, one of the leading creative forces in the modern music business, and tmwrk, his management team and longtime business partners. We’re designed to identify and sign writers early, nurture them through the harsh realities of the music business and allow them to reap the benefits of their creative endeavors. Writers and the music publishers that serve them are at their best when they are in a collaborative mode, and we intend to operate that way internally and externally.”

Diplo, who initially formed Bison before bringing Bergman on as a partner, framed the launch as a response to a moment of creative stagnation in the industry. “At a moment when it feels like music needs an injection of originality, our writers are in a position to shape where music goes next,” he says. “Andrew and I are creating a home for those writers, with a team that knows how to develop them and a company built to back them for the long term.”

Bison is based in New York and Los Angeles. Additional signings will be announced in the coming months.

Black Friday & Cyber Monday Deals Start Weeks Early – Shop by Price, Not Date

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Black Friday falls on Nov. 27 in 2026 and Cyber Monday on Nov. 30, but holiday deals now start in October and run into December. Put a price tracker on each item on your list, and buy when it drops to its recent low, even if that comes before either day.

Philadelphia police gave the day after Thanksgiving its Black Friday name in the early 1960s, for the shopping crowds and traffic jams it brought downtown. The National Retail Federation coined Cyber Monday in 2005, after noticing that people kept shopping online from work the following Monday.

Neither is a single day of deals anymore. Amazon’s Prime Day event in October now brings discounts across U.S. retailers, Adobe says. Walmart’s Early Black Friday Deals run Nov. 12 to 15, and its Cyber Monday deals last through Dec. 2. Adobe’s 2026 forecast puts the top online discounts at 14% in early November, 21% in the days just before Thanksgiving, and 30% over the five days from Thanksgiving through Cyber Monday. December’s top discounts should fall back to 10% to 15%.

Adobe also expects Black Friday to bring the season’s best deals on TVs, toys, apparel, appliances, and furniture, Cyber Monday the deepest discounts on electronics and computers, and Thanksgiving the best deals on sporting goods. Those are forecasts for whole categories, though. Say the TV you want hits its lowest price of the season in mid-November. Black Friday can’t beat that price, and the sale that offered it may be over by then.

Buying early doesn’t lock you in. Walmart takes back most items bought from Oct. 1 through Dec. 31 until Jan. 31, so you can return a mid-November purchase there and buy it again if Black Friday beats its price. At a store with a price adjustment policy, you can ask for the difference back instead.

Gifts you mail have deadlines of their own. For delivery before Christmas within the lower 48 states, USPS recommends sending them by Dec. 17 for Ground Advantage and First-Class Mail, Dec. 18 for Priority Mail, and Dec. 19 for Priority Mail Express.

Set a price alert at each item’s recent low now, so you hear the day the price gets there, whether that’s mid-November or Cyber Monday.

For a tracker that covers the stores on your list, see the best price comparison websites.

Three Iran-backed suspects plotted attacks including RAF Fairford incident, US says

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The US removed all its bombers from the UK's RAF Fairford base last month after a major incident in September that police described as "suspicious"

The US Department of Justice (DOJ) has announced charges against three Iran-backed operatives that it alleges were involved in a string of plots in the US and Europe.

The DOJ said Iranian agents most recently "orchestrated a terrorist plot at an air force base in the United Kingdom", saying they were involved in the major incident in September near RAF Fairford in the UK, where US armed forces were stationed.

The three men have been accused by the DOJ of being involved in 18 attacks and attempted attacks, including in London, Amsterdam and Paris. Some of the incidents were said to be directed by the men over FaceTime.

The DOJ said they acted in the name of the group Harakat Ashab al-Yamin al-Islamiya (HAYI).

Iranian Mohsen Mohajeri, Iraqi national Mohammad Ali al-Hassani and dual Iranian-Iraqi citizen Mohammad Baqer Saad Dawood al-Saadi were charged in an indictment unsealed on Tuesday, the DOJ said.

The DOJ said that most recently, al-Hassani "orchestrated a terrorist plot" at RAF Fairford - where on 27 September a major incident was declared after suspicious vans were reported near the base.

Seven men have been arrested in the UK over the incident at RAF Fairford.

Iran has strongly denied any involvement.

Al-Hassani and Mohajeri, who were allegedly both in Iran at the time of the incident, the indictment claims.

Meanwhile, al-Saadi was coordinating with the pair through third parties from a prison in New York, where he is awaiting trial on previous charges after being arrested in August, it adds.

Mohammad Ali al-Hassani (left) and Mohsen Mohajeri (right) allegedly planned attacks on London from Iraq

Mohajeri and al-Hassani are not in custody, two sources told the BBC's US partner CBS News.

"Of the 18 terrorist attacks in the spring of 2026 for which HAYI claimed responsibility in Europe, seven took place in the UK," the DOJ statement said. It added that the attacks involved "arsons, explosives, and in one instance, a near-fatal stabbing in London of two Jewish men, including a dual US-British citizen".

Two Jewish men were stabbed in Golders Green, north London, in April. A suspect is due to stand trial next year.

The indictment also details an arson attack on a Jewish charity in London on 17 April, which allegedly saw al-Hassani "directing" an individual over FaceTime.

On 17 April , a building previously used by Jewish Futures, an educational charity, was set alight in Hendon, north-west London.

The following day, the DOJ said al-Hassani participated in another video call with an individual carrying out an attack on a synagogue in London.

On 18 April, Kenton United Synagogue in Harrow was targeted in an arson attack. A 17-year-old boy pleaded guilty to the incident earlier this year.

Mohammad Baqer Saad Dawood al-Saadi is currently in prison in New York, awaiting trial for previous charges

The indictment also claims the three were involved in attacks on the Bank of New York Mellon building in Amsterdam on 15 March and on the Bank of America building in Paris on 28 March.

It added that the trio tried to carry out attacks on "Jewish institutions in the US", including attempting to target a synagogue in Manhattan, New York in March or April.

"This eight-count indictment shows the determination of Iran to commit terrorist attacks in Europe and the US," FBI Director Kash Patel said.

He added that one of the three "allegedly sent coded messages to assist in future acts of violence" from detention.

Patel said the accused were involved in nearly 20 attempted attacks in support of Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) and the Iran-backed Iraqi militia Kataib Hezbollah.

Armed police officers were posted outside the RAF Fairford base following the incident in September

A major incident was declared on 27 September near the UK's RAF Fairford base, in the Cotswolds in Gloucestershire, after three suspicious vans were seen near the airbase.

No explosive materials were found in the vans, but a quantity of petrol was seized.

The DOJ said that in August, al-Hassani "allegedly directed and paid associates" in the UK to "conduct preparatory steps and arrange logistics for the RAF Fairford terrorist plot".

On 22 August, al-Hassani paid one individual to "perform pre-operational surveillance of RAF Fairford's perimeter", the department said.

In September, al-Hassani paid several people to help him prepare for the plot, the DOJ also alleged.

According to its statement, this included "procuring vans and barrels of fuel, researching the purchase of chemical accelerants, including large quantities of potassium chloride, and purchasing metallic objects that could be used as explosive shrapnel".

The suspects then gave the vans an appearance of being used by a fuel delivery business and a swimming pool supply business, the DOJ stated.

"On 26 September, al-Hassani allegedly tasked individuals to drive three vans, some of which were later determined to contain barrels of fuel, to the RAF Fairford base."

The DOJ also said al-Hassani was "in frequent communication" with the van drivers and helped to direct them where to drive.

"At least two of the three vans, while en route to the perimeter of RAF Fairford, encountered a security roadblock and were not allowed to proceed on their intended route," it added.

It was in the very early hours of 27 September, that the vans were spotted by a local farmer who was heading home from a party, and called the police.

The Iranian embassy in London has said it "categorically rejects and strongly condemns" what it called "unfounded and malicious speculations" linking Iran to the incident.

Two British nationals appeared in court last month in connection with the alleged Iran-linked plot.

The US withdrew all its bombers from RAF Fairford after the incident, with President Donald Trump citing "some threats" to the base as the reason.

RAF Fairford has been used by the US Air Force (USAF) for decades.

In recent months, the US has flown B-1 and B-52 bombers from there to the Middle East for "defensive" strikes on Iran.

In July, Iran said "any base used for aggression against Iranian territory constitutes a legitimate target for our forces".

Measles is surging in the U.S. — and outbreaks can cost millions

📅 2026-11-11  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Measles outbreaks aren't just a public health threat. They can also be a financial burden on the U.S. — and the cost could only deepen as the once-eliminated disease spreads more widely.

The U.S. is expected to lose its official measles elimination status following a review by the Pan American Health Organization this week, after cases ratcheted to recent highs this year.

More than 5,100 measles cases have been reported nationwide in 2026 as of early November, marking the country's worst outbreak in more than three decades. Eight deaths associated with the disease have been confirmed by states so far in 2026: six in Pennsylvania, one in New York and one in Wisconsin.

Based on this year's case count, a new tool developed by researchers at Johns Hopkins University estimates an economic cost of nearly $83 million for the outbreaks. That total includes $56 million in public health response costs, almost $25 million in lost productivity and more than $1.8 million in direct medical expenses.

The tool also estimates that 2025 measles outbreaks in the U.S. — which involved 2,289 confirmed cases nationwide – carried an economic cost of more than $37 million.

The costs extend beyond treating people who become sick, accounting for the resources health departments spend investigating and containing outbreaks, as well as wages lost when people have to miss work. The tool uses more recent, state-specific cost and financing data than an initial study by the researchers, which relied on historical outbreak costs.

The estimates from the tool are not a definitive accounting of what the outbreaks actually cost. But it illustrates the hefty bill tied to the spread of a disease that can be prevented through vaccination, and the potential economic consequences of falling childhood immunization rates. CNBC previously reported on how the return of measles in the U.S. exposes weaknesses in the nation's public health system that raise concerns about the return of other eliminated diseases.

"We're starting to see a whole bunch of people who decided not to get vaccinated as kids or have never gotten exposed to measles because they benefited from herd immunity, who now make it into adulthood and risk potentially getting quite seriously ill and requiring very significant medical expenses, or dying at the hospital," Bryan Patenaude, a health economist at the International Vaccine Access Center at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, who helped develop the tool, said in an interview.

Measles outbreaks can trigger a costly public health response, even when many people who are exposed never become sick.

When a case is identified, public health officials must investigate the infection, identify and notify people who may have been exposed, coordinate with hospitals and schools, and sometimes organize vaccination campaigns to prevent further spread. Those efforts can divert staff and resources from other public health priorities.

Costs could differ heavily depending on the state.

Johns Hopkins' data includes national averages alongside state-specific inputs, including inpatient hospital costs and wages from government data or trusted health policy sources. In New York, for example, the average inpatient hospital stay is roughly $4,200 a day, while the minimum wage is $16 per hour.

The tool estimates that the 48 cases reported by New York in 2025 carried an estimated $1.1 million in costs, including nearly $749,000 in public health responses, $333,000 in productivity losses and roughly $24,500 in direct medical expenses.

Meanwhile, the 803 cases in Texas, the epicenter of the largest U.S. measles outbreak in 2025, carried estimated costs of nearly $13 million, according to the tool. That includes $9 million in public health costs, $4 million in productivity losses and around $288,000 in direct medical expenses.

The tool builds on a study published by Patenaude and other researchers in August, which examined the costs of measles outbreaks in the U.S. The researchers reviewed published studies and government reports to compile historical estimates of medical expenses and public health response costs across 18 states.

The paper estimated that outbreaks carried a historical average cost of nearly $59,000 per case, just from the public health response. The researchers also estimated how costs scale with outbreak size, separating fixed up-front response expenses from the additional costs associated with each new case.

"A lot of this is incurred up front, and that's because of the human labor that's involved in contact tracing and all of the different actors that sort of need to be mobilized to deal with the fact that there's an outbreak," Patenaude told CNBC. "It's a largely labor-driven cost."

That model estimated roughly $298,000 in fixed costs and about $16,300 for each additional case. But it relied on historical data, including a particularly large 2019 outbreak in New York City, where contact-tracing labor costs were high. The researchers also excluded productivity losses from that model because most of the historical studies they reviewed did not consistently account for them.

The economic burden of measles extends beyond the costs of treating infected patients and organizing a public health response.

People who are exposed to measles may have to quarantine, even if they never develop symptoms. Parents can also be forced to miss work to care for sick or exposed children, while workers who contract the disease may lose wages during their recovery.

A 2025 study by Joey Mattingly, a pharmacist and health economist at the University of Utah, used published epidemiological and cost data to model the potential economic burden of a hypothetical 1,500-case outbreak. His analysis included direct medical expenses, public health spending and lost productivity, and examined how the estimates changed under different assumptions, including hospitalization rates.

The study estimated that the hypothetical outbreak could cost about $90 million, including roughly $26 million in productivity losses.

Those indirect costs can add up quickly because measles is highly contagious and a single infected person can expose dozens of others. Those contacts may need to quarantine or miss work while health officials determine whether they have been infected, creating costs even when they never develop the disease.

Mattingly estimated that each person who is quarantined could amount to roughly $300 in lost productivity alone.

If a single measles case exposes 40 to 50 people who then need to quarantine, that could translate into an estimated $12,000 to $15,000 in lost productivity, even if none of those contacts become sick.

That ripple effect is one reason Mattingly argues that the economic costs of outbreaks can quickly outweigh the investment required to vaccinate people against measles.

"Even if you don't get the infection, the contact costs you," he said. "And so it's just showing that there's this multiplication or exponential growth on the economic side that happens."

The financial burden can also fall unevenly across communities. While the costs of responding to an outbreak may be shared among local, state and federal agencies, Mattingly said the impact is often felt directly by local health departments.

"It's not like your health department is sitting around waiting for measles outbreaks," he said. "They're working, doing all these other things, and imagine having to just drop what you're doing because there's been a huge outbreak in the community."

Patenaude said the findings could help state and local health departments better anticipate the resources needed to contain outbreaks — particularly as measles cases continue to rise.

"It's not super expensive to treat and deal with a single case of measles compared to a single case of cancer," he said. "But if you have these outbreaks popping up and you're having to constantly divert resources that could be spent elsewhere by state and local health departments on measles, then you're going to wind up spending a lot of money very quickly."

3 Winterization Projects That Are as Easy as Painting

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

100+ Most Unique and Practical Gifts 🎁 for Homeowners and DIYers

Published Nov 11, 2026 10:30 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Tips, tricks & ideas for a better home and yard, delivered to your inbox daily.

Winter weather can be devastating to outdoor furniture, appliances, and structures, which is why many people store as much as possible in a covered shed or garage throughout the cold season. Even though not everything can be neatly tucked away, such as your deck, walkways, and gates, you can take steps to prepare them for harsh winter conditions before snow starts to fall.

Outdoor winterization projects that involve painting, staining, or sealing help surfaces create a durable barrier against freeze-thaw cycles, moisture intrusion, and corrosive deicing salts. It’s important to complete these protective jobs before the first hard freeze so you can prevent deep structural damage during the winter months.

Leaving wood decks, fences, or patio furniture bare during winter allows moisture from snow and ice to seep into the wood pores. When trapped water freezes, it expands, causing the wood to split, warp, and rot. A fresh coat of waterproof sealant or oil-based stain acts as a hydrophobic barrier. Water pools on top of the sealed surface rather than penetrating the wood grain.

Before applying a compatible exterior stain or water-repellent finish, clean the surface, remove failing finish, and allow the wood to dry sufficiently. For decking and steps, choose a winterization product specifically approved for horizontal surfaces and foot traffic.

Marc Sharkey, owner of Dynamic Painting Inc, says that “the finish reduces water absorption from rain and melting snow. Repeated wetting and drying can contribute to checking, splitting, and warping; prolonged dampness also creates conditions for decay.

“Pay particular attention to exposed board ends and areas where the old finish has worn away.” He also warns: “A new coating cannot repair rotten wood.” Given that Sharkey’s business is based in Alberta, he has experience dealing with some of the harshest winters in North America.

This oil-based formula provides corrosion resistance and weather protection for outdoor metal surfaces . Learn more in our buyer’s guide.

Winter preparation is important to safeguard your home. Chipped paint on steel or iron railings, furniture, and gates exposes the raw metal to oxygen and winter humidity, rapidly accelerating the oxidation process. Road salt or salt-based sidewalk ice melts further expedite this corrosion, eating through the metal structures. Applying a coat of exterior, rust-resistant enamel or oil-based paint seals out moisture and oxygen, halting the chemical reaction that creates rust.

It’s a budget-friendly winterization project that also forms a slick, chemical-resistant barrier against salt splash-back. Before applying a coating, remove loose rust and flaking paint and clean away dirt and salt. Then apply an appropriate rust-inhibiting primer and exterior metal finish. Check compatibility carefully: steel, aluminum, and galvanized metal can require different preparation and primers.

“An intact coating limits exposure to moisture and oxygen, helping slow corrosion. Left exposed, rust can spread beneath surrounding paint, causing more peeling and pitting. Over time, severe corrosion can weaken components. Painting over loose rust does not solve the underlying problem,” explains Sharkey.

Moisture from ice and snow can cause significant damage to concrete because it is a highly porous material. When snow melts on walkways, driveways, or patios, the concrete absorbs the water. Subsequent freezing temperatures cause that internal water to expand, which leads to surface flaking, scaling, and deep cracking.

Unlike surface coatings, penetrating sealers chemically bond inside concrete pores. They create an invisible, breathable hydrophobic barrier that prevents liquid water from sinking into the stone, while still allowing internal vapor to escape.

Sharkey notes that “for compatible, absorbent concrete steps, walkways, or patios, a penetrating sealer rated for exterior concrete and freeze-thaw exposure can reduce water absorption. Confirm compatibility with any existing treatment and follow the preparation instructions.”

Reducing water and deicing-salt penetration helps protect against winter deterioration. However, just keep in mind that a sealer is preventive maintenance; it does not repair cracks, failing concrete, or drainage problems.

Curb Appeal Updates You’ll Appreciate Even If You Aren’t Selling Your Home

Curb appeal does not have to be solely cosmetic. See which good-looking projects we are prioritizing to make homes safer, more energy-efficient, and weather resistant.

He always ensures readers get the information they need to tackle their next project.

We Decoyed Big Bull Moose in the Alaska High Country

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 11, 2026 10:00 AM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

As we loaded our gear into the bush planes, Bruce Werba offered one last piece of advice. “Be sure to use all 44 stakes. It’ll get windy up there—I guarantee it.”

I’d spent enough time in the Alaskan backcountry over the years that he wasn’t going to get an argument from me.

Our squadron consisted of three Super Cubs with tundra tires. I folded myself into the cramped space behind the pilot where the rest of my gear was stored, my shoulders touching both sides of the fuselage. We lifted off the gravel runway that’s the primary connection between the 160 souls who call Holy Cross, Alaska, home and the rest of the world, and banked over the wide and muddy Yukon River before my pilot, Matt, put us on a westerly bearing.

We were in the air for nearly an hour, heading into the Yukon Delta National Wildlife Refuge, a massive region that borders the Bering Sea and is bigger than the state of Maine. I took mental note of the terrain we flew over, a mix of pine-­covered mountains, tundra, vast patches of willows, and countless bogs, ponds, and rivers. It occurred to me that if, for whatever reason, we had to trek out on foot, we’d probably never make it back.

Over the roar of the engine, Matt shouted that we were approaching our landing zone. He made a large circle over an exposed flat that was surrounded by mountains on three sides to make sure he could put us down safely.

He pointed the nose of the plane into the stiff easterly wind for the final approach, and we bounced to a standstill in a matter of feet. He jumped out of the cockpit and signaled by hand to the other pilots to help them land.

As soon as the three planes were down, the pilots quickly pitched our gear onto the tundra and just as quickly took off. “Good luck,” Matt said before firing up his engine. Time is money for these guys, and they weren’t wasting any of it.

The three of us looked around at the place that would be home for the next 12 days. I was with my longtime friend Jason Nash, who works at Federal Ammunition, and photographer Tom Fowlks. This was Jason’s first moose hunt, and we had decided to do it on our own terms: getting dropped off in the wilderness during the rut and calling bulls.

We had to manhandle our tent, which was bucking in the strong wind, to get it set up. The forecast said the prevailing winds for the next 10 days would be out of the south, so we oriented the entrance toward the north and, with Werba’s admonition in mind, pounded all 44 stakes deep into the rocky soil with large stones.

Werba, who runs Alaska Pike Safaris with his wife, Connie, is a bit of a legend in Alaskan outfitting circles. He has the friendly demeanor of everyone’s favorite grandpa (he’s in his 70s), but is almost inhumanly tough. That spring, a few months before our trip, he’d had a heart attack while guiding pike fishermen and wasn’t able to get to a doctor in Anchorage until four days later—yet he was back on the job in a matter of days, doing work that would grind down a man half his age. His reputation for putting both his guided and self-guided hunters on big bulls is why I sought him out. I’ve been on half a dozen moose hunts over the years and have killed three, but I’d never had a chance on a true monster—one of those gaggers in the mid-60s that just takes your breath away—until now.

While Jason and Tom set up the cots in the tent, I dug a pit and lined it with a tarp that I weighted down with rocks to collect drinking water from the rains that were sure to come. Once the camp was set, we fired up the stove, heated water, and ate freeze-dried spaghetti and beef stroganoff straight from the bag. We had some bourbon to celebrate the start of the adventure, half a bottle disappearing with alarming speed. We’d only purchased two bottles back in Anchorage, a nod to modesty that the three of us instantly regretted. For the rest of the trip, we invoked emergency rationing.

It rained heavily that first night, and the wind beat on our tent, making the rainfly snap and pop like a flag in a gale. The next morning, I fiddled with the guide lines until I got the rigging right, and the tent quieted down.

After downing mugs of instant coffee, we explored our outpost. The broad flat was covered in tundra with patches of gravel and a few small stands of pine and willow. Everywhere I looked I could see bushes laden with ripe blueberries, and dark crowberries carpeted the ground.

Both ends of the flat, which was about three-quarters of a mile long, gave commanding vistas of the country. The north end overlooked a long, broad valley rimmed by mountains that descended to a river about a mile away. To the south, we had views of more mountains and the upper reaches of a pine-forested draw that dropped steeply down to god knows where. I hoped that if a good bull were down there, I could coax it out of the timber—because there was no way on Earth I’d chase one into that hellhole.

A large grizzly came through our camp at lunch. We were back at the tent, comparing notes and eating sandwiches, when he appeared. He angled slowly toward our tent, head down, eating berries. He was a big brute: small ears, broad across his shoulders and rump, sagging belly. I couldn’t tell what his hide would have squared—I’m not particularly good at that—but he was a toad. If I were chasing grizzlies, he’s exactly the kind of bear I’d be looking for.

As it was, the three of us just watched him strut our way. The wind was at his back, and he hadn’t noticed the interlopers on his turf. He dropped down and rolled on his back like an enormous dog, trying to deal with the swarm of bugs around him. We stood there, Jason and I, with rounds chambered in our .30/06s, as he cruised past us at 75 yards. Later that afternoon, while glassing for bulls, I saw him through the spotter about 2 miles away on a mountain, still going.

Our hunting technique wasn’t complicated. We had two cow moose decoys and a makeshift moose paddle that consisted of a white mesh game bag I had stretched over a wire hanger. Because the wind rarely let up, we put one decoy in a small willow-choked gap on the edge of the northern lookout, where any bull to the north or west of us would see it.

From a spot above and behind the decoy, we’d glass while calling every 10 minutes or so. Cupping my hands over my mouth, I’d wail like a lonely estrous moose, emptying my lungs for all they were worth. Sometimes I’d mix in bull calls, a deep, guttural mwah that to me always sounds like an old man grieving some terrible loss.

The wind picked up, and fog and rain rolled in, which cut our visibility to a couple hundred yards but provided relief from the swarms of tiny black flies that pounced whenever the breeze died. We had Thermacells to help with the mosquitos, but they didn’t dissuade those psychotic little bloodsuckers. Only our full-face bug nets and a liberal dousing with pure DEET kept them at bay.

On the fourth day, I spotted my first moose. I was trudging across the tundra, working toward the south end of the flat to glass, when I saw the cow in a small patch of willows. She’d spotted me too, and even though 700 yards separated us, that was too close for her, so she turned around and loped over the hill and out of view. Watching moose cover ground is an impressive sight. Standing still, they are awkward-looking creatures, but nothing moves through rough terrain the way they can, like skaters on ice. They can run through timber blowdowns that would stop any two-legged hunter in their tracks.

I posted up on an exposed knob that was flanked by several large willows, giving me some cover from which to glass the area. At one point, I watched a nervous-­looking black bear work back and forth across the hillside that dropped into the hellhole. He was eating berries in the same patch I’d walked through a couple of days before. Between mouthfuls, he’d pop up on his hind legs and look around before grabbing some more. After 20 minutes, he saw or smelled something he didn’t like and beelined into the depths of the dark timber.

By the end of the day, I was tired and scanning on autopilot. So when I swung my binocular to the right and got a full-frame view of a grizzly walking toward me, the adrenaline surge felt like a physical blow.

Once my lungs and heart started to work in concert again, I took another look. Like the grizzly that had strolled through our camp, he was a big bear, walking with the assured saunter of an apex predator.

He was 300 yards away, and while he wasn’t walking fast, his every step was bringing him right to me. It was getting late, and the light was failing. Even though I was well-armed with both my .30/06 and a .44 on my hip, I wanted to give the bear his space. So in the spirit of General O.P. Smith, commander of the 1st Marine Division at the Chosin Reservoir, who said, “Retreat, hell! We’re not retreating, we’re just advancing in another direction,” I packed my gear and headed back toward the tent, glancing over my shoulder every few seconds to make sure the griz wasn’t closing the gap.

Before I could make it to camp, I spotted Jason and Tom walking toward me. They were both grinning.

Jason told me that earlier that day, he had called in a bull and gotten a good look at him. He judged it at slightly larger than 50 inches. Many places in Alaska impose a 50-inch minimum on nonresidents, but we happened to be in an any-bull area, so his main concern was just making sure it was a mature bull. The most common way to size up a moose’s rack is to wait for it to face you, then compare the width of the antlers to the distance between its eyes.

Measured from the outside corners, a bull moose’s eyes are about 10 inches apart. So, if the antlers extend at least two eye-widths off the side of its head, you’re at 50 inches.

During that first encounter, Jason let the bull melt back into the willows. We still had many days to hunt, the rut hadn’t hit its peak, and the promise of a mythical 65-incher kept his finger off the trigger.

But later, when the bull reappeared, again drawn by Jason’s calls, that logic flew out the window. Jason couldn’t resist, and his 160-yard shot took the bull through the shoulder, and it piled up in a shallow ravine in the willows. His smile told me he had no regrets.

The grizzly that had been trailing behind me vanished, but we still had bears on our mind as we put on our headlamps to recover the bull. The terrain down in the willow thickets is featureless in daylight; at night, it’s a maze.

Eventually, we found the moose and got to work. It was a long, wet night. A pea-soup fog rolled in and gave the kill site an otherworldly feel. We managed to get the hide off, the moose quartered, and the first loads—the rear hinds—strapped to our backs. The fog rendered our headlamps nearly useless, blinding us more than lighting our way.

The hike to camp was less than half a mile, but it was all uphill and through the boggy tundra with more than 130 pounds of meat and bone on our backs. By the time we crested the final hill, my legs were shaking, and it was 5 a.m.

Jason groaned. “That’s some Navy SEAL shit.”

We each took a shot of bourbon and tried to dry off as best as possible before crawling into our sleeping bags, but by this point in the hunt, everything was wet, and our tent smelled like a sweatshop. The next day, we hauled the remaining meat, cape, and antlers back to camp. By then it was raining hard, and our first priority was to protect the venison. Tom took the camp ax and cut down a few small pine trees so we could build a lean-to against the side of the tent to keep the meat dry. We chopped off the boughs to make a bed, and tied sections of the trunks together with paracord to form a frame over which we draped tarps.

After removing the meat from the bags, we dumped a Costco-size container of pepper on it to keep the flies away. We spread the game bags on the branches and put the meat on top of the bags. As the smell of the spiced fresh meat hit my nostrils, I salivated like one of Pavlov’s dogs.

I called Bruce Werba on the sat phone to let him know we had a batch of meat to fly out. But the clouds and fog were keeping all the planes grounded.

When I asked when the conditions might shift, he said, “Be patient.” When I told him about our scant moose sightings, he repeated that mantra. “Be patient. It can change at any moment.”

Of course, we didn’t have a choice in the matter. The weather was going to do what it would do, and the same with the moose. So we went back to glassing as the rain and the temperature fell.

The very next day, I spotted a good bull high on the mountains behind us to the east. He was feeding up a draw to a saddle. I cow-called and he paused but didn’t turn around or look my way. Eventually, he crested the ridge and vanished.

It rained nonstop for the next three days, a cold downpour that finally managed to defeat my Gore-Tex rainwear, making for some truly miserable glassing. Every few hours I’d return to the tent to heat water and make coffee or hot cider to stop the shivering.

At night, our tent offered a fragile haven from the elements, rocking back and forth like a ship in the violent winds that Werba had predicted. The lantern above our heads created a small pocket of warm air at the top of the tent, and we hung our wet gloves and socks around it each night in the hopes they would dry. Much of our conversation turned to how best to stay warm. We stuffed the edges of our sleeping bags with extra clothes, and I started wearing my down jacket as I slept, even though it, too, was wet.

Read Next: A DIY Caribou Hunting Adventure in Alaska

On day 10, I saw two more bulls. The first looked to be in the mid-50s and had three brow tines on each side. He was angling toward the river, and I last saw him at 500 yards as he went into the timber. I figured he’d show again later so I could get a better look at his antlers, but I was wrong—he was gone for good.

At the end of the day, I called in a bull in the low 50s. It had gotten foggy again, so we didn’t have much visibility, but I watched him come my way through the willows as he tipped his rack from one side to the other. He walked right below my perch, almost within bow shot.

That night, another bear came into camp and dragged Jason’s rack away. We found it 50 yards downhill the next morning. A couple of the points on one paddle had been chewed, and we could see where the bear had scraped bits of meat off the skull. Fortunately, the bear hadn’t worked up the courage to raid our stash of meat under the tarp’s lean-to. He had, however, left an impressive pile of brilliant purple scat next to the tent that, in my addled state, looked like the filling for a delicious blueberry cobbler.

The fog that morning was the thickest it had been. We couldn’t see more than a hundred yards in any direction. But this was the last full day of the hunt, so I went to the northern lookout and stared into the mist. Eventually, the clouds lifted, and I glassed a pair of antlers that appeared like the sails on a man-of-war emerging from a fog bank about 800 yards away.

He was with three other moose: a cow, her calf, and a spindly-horned young bull. After a closer look, I realized he was the smaller bull that we’d already called in several times. There was no calling him today, however: His intentions regarding the cow were unmistakable. He separated her from the calf like a cutting horse. She tried to get back to her calf a dozen times, but he was faster and stronger and herded her off into the willows as the younger bull and the calf watched.

That night we polished off the bourbon and ate the final bags of Mountain House lasagna, which we had saved for the occasion. I got Werba on the sat phone the next morning. The good news was the weather had cleared and the Super Cubs could take off from Holy Cross to collect us. More good news came in that his self-guided hunters who had been on the river had done well. One shot a 68-inch bull, and another had dropped a 74-incher.

Werba said he was sorry the big boys hadn’t worked their way up higher, and that none of the mountain hunters had shot toads. The weather had been too warm to get the huge bulls moving, and that was that. Whenever your heart is set on a trophy animal, leaving with an unpunched tag is always a possibility. Truth is, I didn’t mind. Jason got a terrific bull, we’d had the great adventure we came for, and I knew at some point I’d return to the Lower Yukon to try again.

After I hung up with Werba, the three of us pulled the 44 stakes from the ground, packed up, and looked to the sky, waiting to hear the drone of motors of the airplanes that would take us home.

Moose are big animals, but they aren’t especially difficult to bring down. Even moderate cartridges work well on them. In Sweden and Canada, more moose have been killed with the 6.5×55 Swede and the .303 British than anything else, and neither are punishing to shoot. As always, shot placement and bullet construction are the key factors.

On this hunt, Jason and I were both carrying .30/06s. My rifle (above) was built for the elements. I used a stainless Shilen DGR action and Shilen barrel with a Manner’s EH-1A stock and topped it with a 2.5–15×44 Maven RS.1. The rig handled the cold and wet without any problems.

One reason I went with the .30/06 was to try out some of Federal’s new loads. After several years’ hiatus, Federal is working with bullet-makers beyond the Vista Outdoor family of brands. (Vista is Federal’s parent company.) So, once again, hunters can get Federal Premium cartridges topped with bullets from Barnes, Nosler, Hornady, Berger, and others. I’m glad these companies have decided to kiss and make up.

Read Next: I Spent 12 Days Trapped with a Moose Guide Who Wouldn’t Give Up

I shot the 165-grain Barnes TSX, which, thanks to its monolithic construction, plows deep into whatever you’re shooting, smashing through hide and bones, and retains nearly all of its weight.

Jason used a 180-grain Nosler Accubond, which I think is about the best all-around big-game bullet made. It is accurate, mushrooms beautifully, penetrates well, and does everything a bullet is supposed to. Another benefit of these projectiles is they would work well on grizzlies. Just in case.

This story was originally published in a 2019 Issue of Outdoor Life.

Jacobs Reveals De Zerbi Likes Solanke Despite Tottenham's scoring issue

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Roberto De Zerbi is not as panicked about Tottenham's striker issues as many others. Only two Premier League sides have scored less than Spurs so far this season, and there are concerns over summer signing Omar Marmoush, who has not hit the ground running in North London.

Marmoush was supposed to fix Tottenham's lack of goals, but he has just one goal in nine Premier League games, and his performances have been below par. Meanwhile, Dominic Solanke has continued to struggle, as he has done for much of his time at the club, scoring just once himself in 10 league appearances so far this season. Elsewhere, Richarlison has been cast aside.

It has been a disappointing start to the season in general for Spurs, who are down in 11th place despite putting together a record summer transfer window in terms of spending. Spurs did have a bounce back in recent weeks, winning three on the bounce, but they have since lost to Leeds United to start the alarm bells ringing again going into the international break.

With Spurs having been such big spenders in the summer transfer window, the question now becomes whether they will invest again in the January window, which is a notoriously expensive window to do business, especially for key positions like the striker position.

GIVEMESPORT's Ben Jacobs has provided some important insight on the Market Madness podcast.

"What they won't want to do is necessarily go all in and panic buy in January for someone on a long-term contract, instead of just biding their time and waiting it out," he said.

Because you've got Dominic Solanke, who is liked, according to my information, by Roberto De Zerbi.

Hopefully, there's other wider players and versatile attackers that can chip in with goals. And Omar Marmoush is still there as well and can play in more than one position.

"So they're short on goals. But unless you disagree, Kate, they're not actually that short, in my opinion, of attacking options, they just maybe could use a luxury extra number nine option.

"But if everyone's fit, Roberto de Zerbi won't be pressing the panic button. So they could move for a striker in January, but only if the right target presents itself."

Who that right target could be remains to be seen, especially with the prices they are likely to face in the winter window. Top clubs do not want to sell their best strikers in the winter, so it's usually a case of paying a huge fee or settling for less than best, which Spurs would be wise to stop doing.

It is certainly not out of the question that Marmoush comes good, given he has a recent history of scoring Premier League goals for Manchester City. But the narrative is already turning against him, and there is no sign of Solanke becoming a reliable source of goals.

The goals are going to have to come from somewhere, and in the Premier League, they rarely come in the form of a makeshift striker, so Spurs would be wise to look elsewhere if, come January, they feel as though Marmoush and Solanke are not - or will not become - reliable sources of goals.

ITV Unveils “Temporary” Cost Cuts as Q3 Ad Revenue Drops 8 Percent Amid “Weaker” Demand

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The U.K. TV giant, led by CEO Carolyn McCall, this summer agreed to sell its TV channels and streaming service to Comcast's Sky for $2.13 billion, but regulators are now reviewing the deal.

U.K. TV giant ITV, led by CEO Carolyn McCall, reported latest revenue trends for its ITV Studios production arm and advertising business on Wednesday.

Total third-quarter ad revenue fell 8 percent from the year-ago period. That left nine-month ad revenue down 1 percent.

“Due to the weaker demand for advertising, we expect total advertising revenue to be down around 5-10% in Q4 and down around 2-3% for the full year 2026 when compared to the same period in 2025,” McCall said. “To align our costs with advertiser demand, we have identified £30 million of additional temporary savings in M&E in Q4. This comprises: £25 million of content savings and £5 million of discretionary non-content savings.” Related Stories Movies 'Long Long Night' Director Youm Kyu-bock on Creating Animation That Takes Kids Seriously Movies Brandon Sklenar on 'Iron Jane,' Body Dysmorphia and His Rapid Hollywood Rise

The ad business is part of ITV’s Media & Entertainment (M&E) segment, which the company agreed to sell to Comcast’s Sky in July for $2.13 billion. “While the outlook for advertising remains muted, we are firmly focused on self-help and mitigating actions, while continuing to execute our strategic priorities to drive performance across both businesses,” McCall said.

The CEO also commented on the deal outlook, saying: “The agreed sale of our M&E business to Sky, which will position ITV Studios as a highly attractive, pure play, global content business, is progressing as planned,: McCall said. “On 4 November, the Secretary of State issued a Public Interest Intervention Notice (PIIN). This is an expected and important milestone in the regulatory review process, and we are pleased that it is moving at pace. We continue to expect completion in the second half of 2027.”

Last week, the U.K. government filed an intervention notice, meaning U.K. Culture Secretary Lisa Nandy asked regulators Ofcom and the Competition and Markets Authority (CMA) to formally investigate the deal. While Ofcom’s review will focus on the proposed merger’s public interest impact, the CMA will investigate it from a competition perspective.

ITV on Wednesday also disclosed that ITV Studios, which will become a standalone company once the Sky deal closes, recorded a revenue drop of 1 percent for the first nine months of 2026.

“ITV Studios’ performance for the first nine months, with total revenue down 1%, was in line with our expectations,” McCall said. “As previously guided, production deliveries and licensing deals are heavily weighted to Q4 this year and despite this, we have a greater proportion of our full-year Studios revenue secured than at this point last year.”Total ITV revenue fell 1 percent over the first nine months of 2026, with external revenue down 2 percent.

ITV agreed to sell its media and entertainment arm to Sky in a deal that will see it divest its terrestrial TV channels and its streaming service ITVX. Sky is led by CEO Dana Strong. It not only operates pay-TV and streaming businesses in the U.K., Ireland and Italy but has also been growing its telecom offerings, such as broadband and mobile phone operations. Sky also owns the production arm Sky Studios, which is led by Cécile Frot-Coutaz and has been growing its investment in original content creation

Rivian's Vans Got a Serious Upgrade. Could Another Major Fleet Deal Finally Follow?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

There are more reasons for fleet operators to take a second look.

Rivian (RIVN -2.21%) investors can be forgiven for keeping their eyes glued to the R2. The highly anticipated and more affordable electric SUV is central to the company's growth plans, and it has already become Rivian's best-selling vehicle.

But another growth opportunity that has spent some time on the back burner is quietly getting an upgrade. Last month, Rivian began delivering new configurations of its electric delivery van (EDV) that could help attract another substantial fleet order – years after loosening its exclusive arrangement with retail juggernaut Amazon (AMZN +1.48%). Let's dig into what changed, why it matters, and how Amazon still has a say in certain potential deals.

The upgrades include an all-wheel-drive configuration and a larger battery offering 30% more range. Rivian says these additions are designed to expand coverage into more rural delivery networks. That could help existing customers expand their use while giving prospective buyers another reason to take a look. Nobody orders a fleet because the headlights are cute, or if they do, they probably won't find themselves in business long. These vehicles need to earn their parking spaces through creating some kind of value and/or efficiency for the operator. Here are brief reminders of just some of the advantages Rivian's EDVs offer.

First, the potential for energy savings is real. Electricity can cost less per mile than gasoline or diesel, especially if there's well-managed depot charging, including charging at off-peak times. Fuel prices remain elevated compared to a year ago, giving operators reason to run the numbers again.

Second, electric vans eliminate engine oil changes and have fewer moving drivetrain parts, while regenerative braking reduces brake wear. That doesn't eliminate repairs or downtime, but it generally means less routine maintenance. The upgrades could bring the vans' existing advantages to more routes. For fleet operators, those advantages come down to cost. If pilot testing shows prospective buyers that Rivian's EDVs can lower the cost per delivery, the vehicles become compelling.

Rivian changed its agreement with Amazon in late 2023, opening the door to other commercial customers. There has been progress since then, with HelloFresh adding 70 Rivian EDVs, while AT&T (T +0.64%) announced an agreement to purchase Rivian vehicles through a pilot program.

Those are recognizable names, but the next meaningful milestone would be a substantial fleet commitment demonstrating broader demand beyond Rivian's anchor customer, which really put the company on the map for many investors in the early days.

There's also a bit of a catch for Rivian and its investors. Some of the businesses best suited to use Rivian's EDVs could also compete with Amazon. Rivian's latest filing says sales to certain last-mile delivery and retail customers still require Amazon's consent, potentially discouraging those buyers. Rivian's upgraded EDVs could attract more interest, but Rivian doesn't have complete freedom in turning that interest into orders.

For shareholders, another sizable customer would matter beyond the delivery total. It would emphasize that Rivian can build a broader commercial business around a product developed primarily for Amazon. Furthermore, if potential increased demand for its EDVs increases factory output, it could help spread fixed costs beyond its scaling R2 production. Watch for pilot programs turning into larger purchases and existing customers expanding their fleets. I had hoped to see more of that this year, but the upgrades give prospective buyers another reason to take a look.

Rivian's R2 deserves plenty of the attention it's currently receiving. Just don't overlook the delivery van parked beside it -- it may yet have another big order down the road.

Vowles: Williams got carried away with success but 2026 only a blip

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Watch or listen to the latest episode of The Fast and the Curious to hear Williams boss James Vowles discuss the team's 2026 struggles, why he is confident a corner has been turned, and the changes he wants to see made to F1's cost cap rules

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

Williams team principal James Vowles has admitted his squad "got carried away" with their success last season but insists a disappointing 2026 campaign is merely "a blip".

Vowles took charge of Williams after joining from Mercedes at the start of 2023 and appeared to be having a hugely positive impact when the team finished fifth in the 2025 constructors' standings, which was their best finish since 2017.

However, the biggest regulation change in the sport's history, which saw the introduction of both new power units and chassis, has seen Williams regress, leaving drivers Carlos Sainz and Alex Albon hugely frustrated.

They are ninth of the 11 teams with three rounds of the 2026 season remaining and have scored just 12 points, which compares poorly to a final total of 137 a year earlier.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

Appearing on the latest episode of The Fast and the Curious podcast, Vowles said: "There's going to be blips in the pathway. This is what I consider a blip, unfortunately.

"And I think it's a good thing we went through it because we've made some pretty enormous changes in the backside of the team, which we had to go through, in my opinion, to make the next steps.

"And I think we got carried away a little bit with ourselves last year, demonstrating that actually when things aren't changing too much, we have the ability to produce a half decent car. But regulation changes will always expose you."

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

Despite Williams' struggles leading to speculation over the security of Vowles' job, the 47-year-old insists that he is not feeling the pressure.

"When I signed up to this, I had an agreement with the board that we're going to find some horrible things when we start pushing ourselves," Vowles said. "It's not a business where we can go out there and completely predict where we're going to be in different phases.

"But I will dedicate my heart, soul and every hour of my day, as will the team into fixing it when we find it. But I ask in return that there's a support behind us in that way.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

"And I have to say that the board has been outstanding, really, to the extent where I remember in winter, a pretty horrible time in winter, I was working seven day weeks and 16-hour, 18-hour days, it was hard. I called to see all of them. And all it was, was just really a 'we're here behind you. How can we help?' That was it.

"It is not an environment where people are taking this light-heartedly. They know that this is going to be a difficult challenge. I knew it was going to be a difficult challenge. My assurance to the world is everything we are doing is directionally correct for our future."

Wayne Rooney: Man Utd 'Need' to Appoint Jose Mourinho to Replace Carrick

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Mauricio Pochettino is emerging as the leading candidate to replace Michael Carrick at Manchester United, but Red Devils icon Wayne Rooney has his own view on who should replace the 45-year-old at Old Trafford.

It was a surprise to many when Carrick was given a stay of execution by Man United chiefs following successive defeats to Como in the Champions League and at home to Bournemouth in the Premier League, but now it appears as though the Middlesbrough boss is reaching the end of his reign.

Since the loss against the Cherries, Carrick has picked up one point from a possible six available in the English top flight, with United needing a 96th-minute equaliser from Patrick Dorgu to get it against Aston Villa on Sunday.

They did produce a remarkable comeback to beat AS Roma at Old Trafford last week in Europe, having been 2-0 down at half-time, but it wasn't enough to convince Jason Wilcox and Omar Berrada that he should remain in the position for the trip to face Liverpool at Anfield after the international break.

Wilcox and Berrada will now present their recommendation that Carrick is relieved of his duties during meetings in Monaco with Sir Jim Ratcliffe this week, with the club's co-owner and the Glazers having the final say on what happens next.

GIVEMESPORT sources revealed on Monday night that Pochettino was emerging as a leading candidate for the role, and that fact has been backed up by David Ornstein on Tuesday, who claims that the club currently aren't pursuing Eddie Howe, Julian Nagelsmann or Antonio Conte.

GMS understands that all three out-of-work managers have their admirers on the United board, and Fabrizio Romano has confirmed there have been talks with people close to Howe over the role, but it appears as though Pochettino is increasingly the favoured candidate.

United will need to pay $10m (around £7m) to release the former Tottenham boss from his contract with the USMNT, so they must decide whether they are going to do that.

Speaking on the latest episode of the Stick to Football podcast, released on Tuesday, Rooney actually believes a return to Old Trafford for Jose Mourinho is 'exactly what they need'.

The 41-year-old, who is about to take part in 'I'm a Celebrity Get me Out of Here' in Australia, said:

He's probably exactly what Man United need now, if I'm being honest. You can see why he's had the success where he's had it.

"I think he's ruthless, he's selfish and if he's got something to say, he'll say it. But I think, if he had a little bit more within the squad, he would have won (the Premier League)."

Rooney clearly thinks that Mourinho would make a difference at Old Trafford, and has unfinished business with the club after leading them to a second-place finish in the 2017-18 season.

If United were carrying out this search in the summer, like they could have been had they not given Carrick the job on a permanent basis, then the Portuguese boss might have been a viable option.

However, the 63-year-old left Benfica to join Real Madrid instead, and he has made a positive start to life as Los Blancos' manager.

Real currently lie in second position in LaLiga, trailing leaders Barcelona by three points, while they are also second in the Champions League standings, behind Paris Saint-Germain only on goal difference.

There is no possibility that Mourinho will be leaving the Bernabau for a relegation battle in the Premier League, and club chiefs will need to find a suitable alternative elsewhere instead.

As things stand, it appears as though Pochettino is the most likely next United manager, and fans will look at what he achieved at Tottenham Hotspur and hope he can transform their fortunes too.

It sounds as if the big calls have already been made.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Sources: Conor Gallagher set for new Tottenham role under De Zerbi

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Transfer Correspondent AUTHORITY Former Sky Sports and ESPN FC correspondent who specialises in global recruitment, contract negotiations, and high-velocity transfer breaking news. FOCUS Exclusive reporting, domestic and international transfer markets, and elite-level insights. THE INSIGHT Pete utilises a Global Network of Industry Sources to deliver verified, first-to-market reporting. His “Deep-Lens” analysis connects the dots between boardroom strategy and pitch-level recruitment, providing a level of transfer clarity that goes beyond surface-level rumours.

Roberto De Zerbi has plans to experiment with Conor Gallagher's role in his Tottenham side, sources have told Football Insider.

The 26-year-old has been heavily linked with a move away from Spurs in recent weeks, with a host of Premier League clubs believed to be keeping tabs on his situation.

That speculation has grown as a result of his lack of consistent playing time this season, but the midfielder has been forced to play in a number of different positions.

As I revealed on the latest edition of Football Insider's Transfer Insider podcast, De Zerbi must give Gallagher a shot at playing in his preferred role as a number eight.

Having played in a more advanced midfield role in the absence of James Maddison this season, there are concerns that the England man has not had the chance to impress.

That has led to questions over Gallagher's long-term future at Tottenham, but there is a feeling that if he can impress in his favoured position, he will still have a part to play.

Football Insider revealed on 8 November that De Zerbi still has plans for Gallagher to play a key role in his side and is not prepared to sacntion a mid-season exit.

The England international has made eight appearances under De Zerbi this season and started four of those, with the manager believed to be a big fan of his.

However, there have been doubts about his role in the team, alongside calls for him to play in his favoured position as he aims to prove his worth to the manager.

Speaking on the Transfer Insider podcast, I explained: "The issue for Conor Gallagher is that he's maybe not found his best position in that Tottenham side right now.

"He's been playing as a number ten when James Maddison was injured, but I don't think that's his best position or where he wants to be playing.

"He feels he would be better playing as a number eight in that midfield, a bit deeper than he's been used at times so far this season.

"Gallagher is hoping he can get a bit of a run in that role and that De Zerbi will give him the chance to impress in his preferred position, rather than where he's been used so far.

"I'm sure Roberto De Zerbi doesn't want to lose somebody like Conor Gallagher as well because such a versatile player."

Despite an expectation that Gallagher will remain a Tottenham player beyond the January transfer window, little has been done to deter interest in his signature.

The midfielder is on the radar of a number of Premier League clubs, including the likes of Everton, Leeds and Aston Villa, who have all shown an interest in recent days.

Meanwhile, Football Insider revealed on 6 November that Crystal Palace are keen on a reunion with Gallagher and could look to lure him back to Selhurst Park.

Palace have been keen to re-sign the midfielder since he left the club following a loan spell in 2022, where many believe he enjoyed the best spell of his career to date.

The Eagles missed out on signing Gallagher last January, but remain keen amid a belief that the midfielder could be tempted away, despite Tottenham's stance.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: helping a sibling [receive financial assistance, but responsible for taxes]

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I gift stock shares to my 2 sons from my Schwab account to each of their Schwab accounts. There's a form that you can find on their site, but you'll want to send them a letter (a picture is fine) to them to create a path directly to the person you want to gift. Once this is done, in your accounts, there will be an account labeled SLOA-Schwab One Acct ### with ### to identify this account. I can very easily and quickly gift cash or shares by doing this. You can set your account default so that shares you want to gift specifically can be designated. I do FIFO (first in first out) so the oldest shares, which are long term get gifted. Remember the default if you sell shares through regular trade and if needed, during the trade process, change the default share designation to what you want to. This does not change the default.

The receiver gets the shares and just like any Schwab account, when you see the stock or fund name, the cost basis, in blue is clickable and that brings up a list of each lot with cost basis, date of purchase, long/short term, gain, etc. I find that Schwab's site has everything I could possibly want to know.

When Schwab generates its consolidated 1099, it will show the sale of any of these shares they have sold and the information can be used for tax preparation.
